<!-- definition-1: body_md -->
\label{def:llr}
Fix nonzero evaluation points $\alpha_1, \ldots, \alpha_n \in \mathbb{F}_p^{\times}$ and let $C \subset \mathbb{F}_p^n$ be the linear code\footnote{A \emph{linear code} is just an $\mathbb{F}_p$-linear subspace of $\mathbb{F}_p^n$.}
\begin{equation}
 \label{eq:secret-zero-code}
 C:= \{(\alpha_i Q(\alpha_i))_{i=1}^n \,:\, \deg(Q) < k \}.
\end{equation}
 For $v \in \mathbb{F}_p^n$, let $L_v$ denote the distribution of the \emph{transcript} $(\ell_1(c_1), \ldots, \ell_n(c_n))$, when $c$ is chosen uniformly at random from the coset $v + C$.
 We say that (the secret-sharing scheme defined by) $C$ is \emph{$b$-bit Local Leakage Resilient (LLR)} if, for any \emph{leakage maps} $\ell_i:\mathbb{F}_p \to \{0,1\}^b$, and for any pair of secrets $s,s' \in \mathbb{F}_p$, 
 \[ d_{\mathrm{TV}}(L_{s\mathbf{1}}, L_{s'\mathbf{1}}) \leq e^{-\Omega(n)}.\]
<!-- end definition-1 -->
<!-- question-1: body_md -->
\label{q:llr}
What is the smallest rate $R^*$ so that, for all $R > R^*$, Shamir's scheme over a prime field $\mathbb{F}_p$ with $k/n = R$ is one-bit LLR?
<!-- end question-1 -->
<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{thm:main-llr}
Every Shamir secret sharing scheme with rate $R$ over a prime field is
\begin{enumerate}
    \item $b$-bit locally leakage resilient for every fixed $b$ and every fixed $R>1/2$;
    \item one-bit locally leakage resilient whenever
    $R>1/2-\delta$, for some absolute constant $\delta >0$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
We emphasize that \cref{thm:main-llr} applies for \emph{arbitrary} leakage functions (in particular, worst-case and not necessarily ``balanced,'' meaning that $\ell_i^{-1}(1)$ may have any size between $0$ and $p$).  However, it still improves on the best known result in the balanced case (when $|\ell_i^{-1}(1)| \sim p/2$ for all $i$).  This result was due to \cite{kk23}, and established $R^* \leq 0.58$ in the balanced case.
    In fact, as part of our proof, we show the balanced case is in fact the \emph{hardest}, in the sense that the sufficient rate $R^*$ threshold in the balanced case hold for arbitrary biases.
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- definition-2: body_md -->
\label{def:opi}
Fix distinct evaluation points $\alpha_1,\ldots,\alpha_n\in\mathbb{F}_p$ and nonempty input lists $S_1,\ldots,S_n\subset\mathbb{F}_p$. The OPI problem asks for a polynomial $Q \in \mathbb{F}_p[X]$ with $\deg(Q) < k$ that maximizes the \emph{satisfaction ratio}
\[
s(Q):=\frac1n\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft|\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft\{i:Q(\alpha_i)\in S_i\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright\}\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright|.
\]
We will often refer to $R := k/n$ as the \emph{rate} of the OPI instance.\footnote{We use this terminology as $R$ is the rate of the corresponding Reed--Solomon code.}
<!-- end definition-2 -->
<!-- definition-3: body_md -->
\label{def:disc}
Let $C\subset\mathbb{F}_p^n$, and fix $S_1, \ldots, S_n \subset \mathbb{F}_p$. Let $\rho_i:=|S_i|/p$ and let $\mathbf{S}:=S_1\times\cdots\times S_n$. We say that $C$ has \emph{near-optimal OPI discrepancy} for input lists $S_1,\ldots,S_n$ if
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:def-perfect-opi}
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft|\frac{|C\cap \mathbf{S}|}{|C|\prod_{i=1}^n\rho_i}-1\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright|
\le e^{-\Omega(n)}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end definition-3 -->
<!-- question-2: body_md -->
\label{q:OPI}
For a fixed rate $R = k/n$ and fixed $\rho \in (0,1)$, what is the largest  $s^* \in (0,1]$ so that every OPI instance of rate $R$ with $|S_i|\ge \rho p$ for all $i$ admits a solution $Q \in \mathbb{F}_p[X]$ with satisfaction ratio $s(Q) \geq s^*$?  What is the largest fraction $s^*_{\mathrm{alg}}  \in (0,1]$ so that a solution $Q \in \mathbb{F}_p[X]$ with $s(Q) \geq s^*_{\mathrm{alg}}$ can be found efficiently with a quantum algorithm?
<!-- end question-2 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
\label{thm:main-algo}
For any $R, \rho, \varepsilon\in (0, 1)$, 
there exists a quantum algorithm with $\operatorname{poly}(n, \log p)$ running time that, given any OPI instance over $\mathbb{F}_p$ with rate $R$ and coherent membership oracle access to input lists $S_i$ where $|S_i|\ge \rho p$ for every $i$, has the following guarantees with high probability:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item Outputs a solution polynomial $Q$ with satisfaction ratio $s(Q)\ge \mathsf{SCL}_\rho(R)-\varepsilon$, 
    \item If $R+\rho>1$, outputs a solution polynomial $Q$ with $s(Q)=1$, i.e. $Q$ satisfies every constraint.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- remark-2: body_md -->
\label{rem:access}
    \cref{thm:main-algo} guarantees a quantum algorithm with membership oracle access to the lists $S_i$.  If the size of the field $\mathbb{F}_p$ is $p = \operatorname{poly}(n)$, then no membership oracles are needed, as the algorithm can simply take the lists $S_i$ as input and be its own oracle.  However, our results also work when $p$ is much larger than $\operatorname{poly}(n)$, in which case coherent membership oracle access is needed for an efficient algorithm. The precise computation model is similar to that of \cite{yz,hy} and is given in \cref{def:input-model}.
<!-- end remark-2 -->
<!-- theorem-3: body_md -->
\label{thm:main-opi}
Fix any $\rho \in (0, 1)$. Every Reed--Solomon code of rate $R$ over a prime field has near-optimal OPI discrepancy for every instance with $|S_i|\ge \rho p$ for every $i$, provided that at least one of the following holds:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item $R>1/2$;
    \item  $R>1/2-\delta$ and $\rho\ge1/2-\delta$, for some absolute constant $\delta >0$;
    \item $R+\rho>1$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end theorem-3 -->
<!-- theorem-4: body_md -->
\label{thm:main-existential}
Fix $\rho\in(0,1/2)$ and $\varepsilon>0$. For sufficiently large $n$, every Reed--Solomon code
$C\subset\mathbb{F}_p^n$ of fixed rate $R\in(\rho, 1/2]$ over a prime
field with $p\ge n$ admits, for every instance with $|S_i|\ge\rho p$
for all $i$, a solution $Q\in\mathbb{F}_p[X]$ with $ \deg Q < k$ with satisfaction ratio $s(Q)\ge V_\rho(R)-\varepsilon$, where
\begin{equation}
    V_\rho(R):=1-(1-2\rho)(1-2R).
\end{equation}
<!-- end theorem-4 -->
<!-- theorem-5: body_md -->
\label{thm:main-opi-advantage}
There exists a random ensemble of balanced OPI instances of length $n$ with rate $1/\mathrm{polylog}(n)$ over prime fields $\mathbb{F}_p$ with quasipolynomial $p = \exp({\mathrm{polylog}(n)})$ 
such that the following hold, given access to a membership oracle for the input lists $S_i$:
\begin{itemize}
\item There is a polynomial-time quantum algorithm that finds a perfect solution with all but negligible probability.
\item Every randomized classical
algorithm making polynomially many queries to the membership oracle has negligible probability of outputting a solution with satisfaction ratio $\frac12+\Omega\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(\frac{1}{\log\log n}\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)$.
\end{itemize}
<!-- end theorem-5 -->
<!-- theorem-6: body_md -->
\label{thm:informal-opi-harder}
Let $\ell_1, \ldots, \ell_n:\mathbb{F}_p \to \{0,1\}$ be one-bit leakage functions.  For $z \in \{0,1\}^n$, define $S_i(z_i) = \ell_i^{-1}(z_i)$.  Let 
$C = \{ \alpha_i \cdot Q(\alpha_i) \,:\, \deg(Q) < k \}$ be the generalized Reed--Solomon code in \cref{eq:secret-zero-code}.  Suppose that, for all $s \in \mathbb{F}_p$, and for all $z \in \{0,1\}^n$,  $s\mathbf{1} + C$ has near-optimal OPI discrepancy for input lists $S_1(z_1), \ldots, S_n(z_n)$.
Then $C$ is 1-bit local leakage resilient.
<!-- end theorem-6 -->
<!-- remark-3: body_md -->
\label{rem:org}
    We organize the technical overview into these six steps, as these are the six conceptual steps needed to prove our results.  However, we organize the paper itself slightly differently.  While Steps 2 and 3 are conceptually similar for LLR and OPI, the details differ, so we instead organize the paper to separate LLR and OPI, as follows. After some preliminaries in \cref{sec:prelim}, we complete Step 1 in \cref{sec:proxies}.  Then we complete Steps 2 and 3 for LLR in \cref{sec:llr}.  After that, we complete Steps 2 and 3 again for OPI, as well as the non-algorithmic part of Step 5, in \cref{sec:opi}.  We translate our combinatorial results into algorithmic results (Step 4 and the algorithmic part of Step 5) in \cref{sec:algo}.  Finally, we handle Step 6 in \cref{sec:advantage}.
<!-- end remark-3 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
\label{lem:wftl}
Fix constants $\gamma \in (0, 1)$ and $\Gamma>0$.  Let $p \geq n$ and let $C \subset \mathbb{F}_p^n$ be a generalized Reed--Solomon code of dimension $k$. Suppose that the weights $w_i:\mathbb{F}_p^\times \to \mathbb{R}_{\ge 0}$ for every $i\in [n]$ satisfies that
\[ \Vert w_i\Vert_\infty\le \gamma+o(1)\quad\text{and}\quad\Vert w_i\Vert_2^2=\sum_{\xi\ne 0}w_i(\xi)^2\le \Gamma+o(1)\] with both errors uniformly in $i$. Then, for every $t\in [n]$ with $2k-t=\Omega(n)$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:wftl}
\sum_{0\ne y\in C^\perp:|y|\le t} \prod_{i:y_i\ne 0} w_i(y_i)\le e^{o(n)} \gamma ^k=e^{-\Omega(n)}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- definition-4: body_md -->
\label{def:list-recovery}
A code $C\subset\mathbb{F}_p^n$ is \emph{$(\delta,\ell,L)$-list recoverable} if, for
any coordinate lists $V_1,\ldots,V_n\subset\mathbb{F}_p$ with
$|V_i|\le\ell$, at most $L$ words $y\in C$ satisfy $y_i\in V_i$ on at
least $(1-\delta)n$ coordinates.
\emph{List decoding} is the singleton-list case: a code is $(\delta,L)$-list
decodable if every Hamming ball of radius $\delta n$ contains at most
$L$ codewords.
<!-- end definition-4 -->
<!-- theorem-7: body_md -->
\label{thm:rs-ld}
For $\varepsilon\in(0,1)$ and all sufficiently large $n$ and prime $p\ge n$, every generalized Reed-Solomon code $C\subset\mathbb{F}_p^n$ with dimension $1\le k\le(1-\varepsilon)n$ admits a deterministic algorithm that, given any $z\in\mathbb{F}_p^n$, in $n^{O_\varepsilon(1)}\widetilde O(\log p)$ running time (uniformly in $k$ and the evaluation places) outputs every $c\in C$ such that
\[
|c-z|\le\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(1-\frac{k}{n}-\varepsilon\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)n.
\]
<!-- end theorem-7 -->
<!-- theorem-8: body_md -->
\label{thm:rs-lr}
For $\varepsilon\in(0,1)$, $\ell\in\mathbb{N}$, and all sufficiently large $n$ and prime $p\ge n$, every generalized Reed-Solomon code $C\subset\mathbb{F}_p^n$ with dimension $1\le k\le(1-\varepsilon)n$ is
\[
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(1-\frac{k}{n}-\varepsilon,\ell,n^{O_{\varepsilon,\ell}(1)}\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)
\text{-list recoverable}.
\]
The exponent is uniform in $p,k$ and the evaluation
points.
<!-- end theorem-8 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
\label{lem:indicator-L-infty-bound}
Let $S\subset\mathbb{F}_p$ have size $|S|=\rho p$.  Then, for every
$\xi\ne0$, as $p\to\infty$ uniformly in $\rho$, we have that
\[
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left|\widehat{\mathbf{1}_S}(\xi)\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right|
\le
\frac{\sin(\pi\rho)}{p\sin(\pi/p)} = \frac{\sin(\pi\rho)}{\pi}+O(p^{-2}).
\]
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- corollary-1: body_md -->
\label{cor:L-infty-bound}
For any $\rho\in(0,1)$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:L-infty-bound}
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left|\widehat g(\xi)\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right|
\le
\gamma(\rho)+O(p^{-2})
\end{equation}
for every $\xi\ne 0$, where
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:def-gamma}
g(x):=\frac{\mathbf{1}_S(x)-\rho}{\sqrt{\rho(1-\rho)}}
\quad\text{and}\quad
\gamma(\rho):=
\frac{\sin(\pi\rho)}{\pi\sqrt{\rho(1-\rho)}}.
\end{equation}
Moreover, setting $\gamma(0)=\gamma(1)=0$ by continuity,
$\gamma:[0, 1]\to [0, \infty)$ is increasing on $(0,1/2]$, decreasing on
$[1/2,1)$, and $\gamma(1/2)={2}/{\pi}$.
<!-- end corollary-1 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
For $\xi\ne 0$, if $h(x) = \rho$ is a constant function then $\hat{h}(\xi) = 0$. In general,  \cref{lem:indicator-L-infty-bound} gives
\[
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft|\widehat{g}(\xi)\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright|\le \frac{\sin(\pi\rho)}{p\sin (\pi/p)\sqrt{\rho(1-\rho)}}=\frac{\gamma (\rho)\cdot \pi}{p\sin(\pi/p)}=\gamma(\rho)+O(p^{-2}),
\]
by linearity of the Fourier transform.
Now, monotonicity follows by differentiating.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- definition-5: body_md -->
\label{def:gowers-norms}
For an integer $s\ge1$ and a function $F:\mathbb{F}_p\to\mathbb{C}$, define
\[
  \|F\|_{U^s}^{2^s}
  :=
  \mathbb{E}_{x,h_1,\ldots,h_s}
  \prod_{\omega\in\{0,1\}^s}
  \mathcal C^{|\omega|}F(x+\langle \omega, h\rangle),
\]
where $\mathcal C$ denotes complex conjugation and all variables are uniform
on $\mathbb{F}_p$.  In particular,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fourierU}
        \|F\|_{U^1}=|\mathbb{E} F|=|\widehat{F}(0)|,
        \qquad
        \|F\|_{U^2}^4
        =\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{F}_p}|\widehat F(\xi)|^4.
\end{equation}
<!-- end definition-5 -->
<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
\label{lem:u3-gap}
For every fixed $\tau\in(0,1/2)$ there is $q<1$ such that the
following holds for all sufficiently large primes $p$. If
$f:\mathbb{F}_p\to\mathbb{R}$ satisfies that
\[
|f(x)|-1=o(1)\quad\text{and}\quad 
    |\mathbb{E}_{x\in\mathbb{F}_p} f(x)|\le 1-2\tau+o(1),
\]
uniformly over $x\in\mathbb{F}_p$, then
$\|f\|_{U^3}\le q$.
<!-- end lemma-3 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
We first claim that it suffices to consider $\pm1$-valued $f$ with no
$o(1)$ errors. Let $\widetilde f:\mathbb{F}_p\to\{-1,1\}$ be a nearest-sign
function, so $\varepsilon
    =\max_{x\in\mathbb{F}_p}\bigl||f(x)|-1\bigr|=o(1)$.
Then, $|\mathbb{E}\widetilde f|  \le |\mathbb{E} f|+\varepsilon \le 1-\tau$
for all sufficiently large $p$.  Applying the $\pm 1$-valued statement
with parameter $\tau/2$ gives
$\|\widetilde f\|_{U^3}\le q_0$ for some fixed $q_0<1$.  Hence,any fixed $q\in(q_0,1)$ works for all sufficiently large $p$ as
\[\|f\|_{U^3}
    \le \|\widetilde f\|_{U^3}
       +\|f-\widetilde f\|_{U^3}
    \le q_0+\varepsilon.\] 

It therefore remains to prove the exact $\pm1$-valued case, i.e. $f:\mathbb{F}_p\to\{-1,1\}$ such that
$|\mathbb{E} f|\le1-2\tau$. Let $\mu:=\mathbb{E} f\in [2\tau-1, 1-2\tau]$.
Fix any $c\in(2/\pi,1)$. By
\cref{lem:indicator-L-infty-bound}, for all sufficiently large $p$, $\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left|\widehat f(\xi)\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right|\le c$
whenever $\xi\ne0$.
Parseval and the Fourier formula in \cref{eq:fourierU} for the $U^2$ norm give
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:u2-gap-bd}  
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft\lVertf\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright\rVert_{U^2}^4 =\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{F}_p}\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left|\widehat f(\xi)\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right|^4 \le|\mu|^4+c^2\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(1-|\mu|^2\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right).
\end{equation}

Since $f$ is $\pm1$-valued, let $f_h(x)=\Delta_h f(x)=f(x+h)f(x)$ for $h\in\mathbb{F}_p$ and let $m_h :=\mathbb{E} f_h$. Then, 
$f_h$ is again $\pm1$-valued, so $|m_h|^4\le |m_h|^2$ and the same argument as \cref{eq:u2-gap-bd} gives
\[
\|f_h\|_{U^2}^4
\le |m_h|^4+c^2\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(1-|m_h|^2\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)
\le c^2+\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(1-c^2\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)|m_h|^2.
\]
Moreover,
we have by \cref{eq:fourierU,eq:gowers_recursion} that
\[
     \|f\|_{U^2}^4= \mathbb{E}_h \|\Delta_h f\|_{U^1}^2
=\mathbb{E}_h |\mathbb{E} \Delta_h f|^2 = \mathbb{E}_h |m_h|^2,
\]
so $\mathbb{E}_h |m_h|^2 = \|f\|_{U^2}^4$.
Together, we have
\[
\|f\|_{U^3}^8 = \mathbb{E}_h \|f_h\|_{U^2}^4
\leq c^2 + (1 - c^2)\mathbb{E}_h|m_h|^2
= c^2 + (1 - c^2)\|f\|_{U^2}^4
\le c^2+\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(1-c^2\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left[|\mu|^4+c^2\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(1-|\mu|^2\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right],
\]
where the first equality follows from \cref{eq:gowers_recursion}.
Since $|\mu|\le 1-2\tau$, $|\mu|^4\le |\mu|^2\le (1-2\tau)^2$, so 
\[ \|f\|_{U^3}^8 \le c^2+(1-c^2)[c^2+(1-c^2)(1-2\tau)^2]=1-(1-c^2)^2[1-(1-2\tau)^2] <1\]
Therefore, choosing $q:=(1-(1-c^2)^2[1-(1-2\tau)^2])^{1/8}<1$ suffices.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- lemma-4: body_md -->
\label{lem:elem-cs}
For any $G, P:\mathbb{F}_p^k\to\mathbb{C}$,
suppose
$G$ is $K$-invariant for some subspace $K\le\mathbb{F}_p^k$, i.e. $G(x+h)=G(x)$ for every $x\in\mathbb{F}_p^k$ and $h\in K$, 
and $G$ is $1$-bounded, i.e. $\|G\|_\infty\le1$.  Then
\begin{equation}
    \mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left|\mathbb{E}_{x\in\mathbb{F}_p^k}G(x)P(x)\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right|^2
   \le
    \mathbb{E}_{\substack{x\in\mathbb{F}_p^k\\h\in K}}
    P(x)\overline{P(x+h)}=: \mathbb{E}_{\substack{x\in\mathbb{F}_p^k\\h\in K}} \Delta_h P(x).
\label{eq:block-fiber-cs}
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-4 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
For any $x\in\mathbb{F}_p^k$ and $h\in K$, $\mathbb{E}_x G(x)P(x+h)=\mathbb{E}_y G(y)P(y)$ for $y=x+h$ by $K$-invariance. Therefore, $\mathbb{E}_xG(x)P(x)=\mathbb{E}_x G(x)\mathbb{E}_{h\in K}P(x+h)$, so by Cauchy-Schwarz,
\[
 \mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left|\mathbb{E}_{x\in\mathbb{F}_p^k}G(x)P(x)\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right|^2 \le \mathbb{E}_x |G(x)|^2\cdot \mathbb{E}_x \mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left|\mathbb{E}_hP(x+h)\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right|^2\le \mathbb{E}_{x}\mathbb{E}_{h, h'\in K}P(x+h)\overline{P(x+h')}
\]
by $1$-boundedness of $G$. Now, setting $y=x+h\in\mathbb{F}_p^k$ and $w=h'-h\in K$ rewrites the right hand side as $\mathbb{E}_y P(y)\overline{P(y+w)}$, which is the desired bound up to renaming the variables. The final equality follows the definition of $\Delta_h$.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- lemma-5: body_md -->
\label{lem:block-u3}
Let $1\le k\le n$, and let
$F_1,\ldots,F_n:\mathbb{F}_p^k\to\mathbb{F}_p$ be linear maps such that $(F_i(x))_{i\in S}:\mathbb{F}_p^k\to\mathbb{F}_p^S$
is an isomorphism for every
$S\in\binom{[n]}{k}$. Let $J\subset[n]$ satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{eq:block-u3-dimension}
    |J|\le\frac{3k-n}{2}.
\end{equation}
Then, for arbitrary functions $L_1,\ldots,L_n:\mathbb{F}_p\to\mathbb{C}$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:block-u3}
    \mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft|\mathbb{E}_{x\in\mathbb{F}_p^k}\prod_{i=1}^n L_i(F_i(x))\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright|
    \le
    \mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(\prod_{j\in J}\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft\lVertL_j\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright\rVert_{U^3}\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)
    \mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(\prod_{i\in[n]\setminus J}\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft\lVertL_i\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright\rVert_\infty\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right).
\end{equation}
The same conclusion holds if each $F_i(x)$ is replaced by
$F_i(x)+c_i$ for an arbitrary $c_i\in\mathbb{F}_p$.
<!-- end lemma-5 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
First, it suffices to show the case where $F_i$ is linear without the affine shift by $c_i$ and that $L_i$ is $1$-bounded  for every $i\in[n]\setminus J$, i.e. $\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft\lVertL_i\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright\rVert_\infty\le1$.
Indeed, if $\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft\lVertL_i\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright\rVert_\infty=0$ for some $i\notin J$, then the
left-hand side of \cref{eq:block-u3} is zero. Otherwise, replace each
$L_i$ with $L_i/\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft\lVertL_i\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright\rVert_\infty$ for $i\notin J$ and restore the
factor $\prod_{i\notin J}\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft\lVertL_i\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright\rVert_\infty$ at the end to prove the general $L_i$ case. Now, suppose that $F_i(x)$ is replaced by $F_i(x)+c_i$. Apply the
linear case to $\widetilde L_i(t):=L_i(t+c_i)$. Observe that
translations preserve both relevant norms $ \mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft\lVert\widetilde L_i\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright\rVert_{U^3}
    =
    \mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft\lVertL_i\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright\rVert_{U^3}$ and $\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft\lVert\widetilde L_i\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright\rVert_\infty
    =
    \mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft\lVertL_i\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright\rVert_\infty$,
which proves the translated statement.


Let $d:=|J|$. Since $n-d\le3(k-d)$, partition
$[n]\setminus J=I_1\sqcup I_2\sqcup I_3$ with $|I_a|\le k-d$.
Define
\[
    F_J(x):=(F_j(x))_{j\in J},\qquad
    H(z):=\prod_{j\in J}L_j(z_j),\qquad
    G_a(x):=\prod_{i\in I_a}L_i(F_i(x)),
\]
so $G_a$ is $1$-bounded since each $L_i$ for $i\not\in J$ is. The quantity whose norm we need to control is
\[
    \Lambda
    :=
    \mathbb{E}_{x\in\mathbb{F}_p^k}\prod_{i=1}^nL_i(F_i(x))
    =
    \mathbb{E}_{x\in\mathbb{F}_p^k}H(F_J(x))G_1(x)G_2(x)G_3(x).
\]

Fix any $a\in[3]$. Observe that $G_a$ is
$K_a$-invariant where $K_a:=\bigcap_{i\in I_a}\ker F_i$. We show that $F_J|_{K_a}:K_a\to\mathbb{F}_p^J$ is surjective. Indeed, coordinates of the map $(F_i)_{i\in J\cup I_a}:\mathbb{F}_p^k\to \mathbb{F}_p^{J}\times F_p^{I_a}$ are independent by the MDS assumption, so the above map is surjective. In particular, for every $z\in \mathbb{F}_p^J$, there exists $y\in \mathbb{F}_p^k$ such that $(F_i)_{i\in J\cup I_a}(y)=(z, 0)$. Hence, $F_J(y)=z$ and $y\in K_a$, as claimed.

Now, we apply \cref{lem:elem-cs} three times.
We will use the following simple observations: first, $\Delta_h(PQ)=\Delta_hP\Delta_hQ$; second, if $G$ is
$K$-invariant and $1$-bounded, then $\Delta_hG$ is also $K$-invariant
and $1$-bounded for every $h\in\mathbb{F}_p^k$. Indeed, for every $u\in K$,
\[
    \Delta_hG(x+u)
    =
    \overline{G(x+u+h)}{G(x+u)} =
    \overline{G(x+h)}{G(x)}
    =
    \Delta_hG(x),
\]
and
\[
    |\Delta_hG(x)|
    =
    |G(x+h)|\,|G(x)|
    \le 1.
\]
The same therefore holds for any iterated derivative of $G$.
Apply \cref{lem:elem-cs} first with $G=G_1$ and $P=(H\circ F_J)G_2G_3$.
Since $G_1$ is $K_1$-invariant and $1$-bounded, we obtain
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:block-cs-one}
    |\Lambda|^2
    \le \mathbb{E}_{\substack{x\in\mathbb{F}_p^k\\h_1\in K_1}} \Delta_{h_1}\bigl((H\circ F_J)G_2G_3\bigr)(x) =\mathbb{E}_{\substack{x\in\mathbb{F}_p^k\\h_1\in K_1}}
    \Delta_{h_1}(H\circ F_J)(x)
    \Delta_{h_1}G_2(x)
    \Delta_{h_1}G_3(x).
\end{equation}

Now, for each fixed $h_1\in K_1$, the function $\Delta_{h_1}G_2$ is
$K_2$-invariant and $1$-bounded. Applying \cref{lem:elem-cs} with
$G=\Delta_{h_1}G_2$
and $ P=
    \Delta_{h_1}(H\circ F_J)\Delta_{h_1}G_3$, and Cauchy-Schwarz over $h_1$ yields
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:block-cs-two}
    |\Lambda|^4
    \le
    \mathbb{E}_{\substack{x\in\mathbb{F}_p^k\\
                  h_1\in K_1,\ h_2\in K_2}}
    \Delta_{h_2}\Delta_{h_1}(H\circ F_J)(x)
    \Delta_{h_2}\Delta_{h_1}G_3(x).
\end{equation}

For each fixed $h_1,h_2$, the function
$\Delta_{h_2}\Delta_{h_1}G_3$ is $K_3$-invariant and $1$-bounded.
Applying \cref{lem:elem-cs} one final time and Cauchy-Schwarz over $(h_1, h_2)$ gives
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:block-three-cs}
    |\Lambda|^8
    \le
    \mathbb{E}_{\substack{x\in\mathbb{F}_p^k\\
                  h_i\in K_i,\ i\in[3]}}
    \Delta_{h_3}\Delta_{h_2}\Delta_{h_1}
    (H\circ F_J)(x).
\end{equation}

It remains to identify the right-hand side with the $U^3$-norm of
$H$.  Since $F_J:\mathbb{F}_p^k\to\mathbb{F}_p^J$ and each restriction
$F_J|_{K_i}:K_i\to\mathbb{F}_p^J$ are surjective linear maps, independent
uniform $x\in\mathbb{F}_p^k$ and $h_i\in K_i$ give independent uniform
variables in $\mathbb{F}_p^J$ that we denote by $ z:=F_J(x)$ and $ u_a:=F_J(h_a)$ for $a\in [3]$. Moreover, by linearity of $F_J$,
$\Delta_{h_3}\Delta_{h_2}\Delta_{h_1}
    (H\circ F_J)(x)=\Delta_{u_3}\Delta_{u_2}\Delta_{u_1}H(z)$.
Therefore, by the definition of the $U^3$-norm, the right-hand side of \cref{eq:block-three-cs} is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:block-rhs}
    \mathbb{E}_{z,u_1,u_2,u_3\in\mathbb{F}_p^J}
    \Delta_{u_3}\Delta_{u_2}\Delta_{u_1}H(z)
    =
    \mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft\lVertH\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright\rVert_{U^3(\mathbb{F}_p^J)}^8,
\end{equation}
Finally, since
the cube average factors over the coordinates $j\in J$, we see that
\[
    H(z)=\prod_{j\in J}L_j(z_j)\implies \mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft\lVertH\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright\rVert_{U^3(\mathbb{F}_p^J)}^8
    =
    \prod_{j\in J}\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft\lVertL_j\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright\rVert_{U^3(\mathbb{F}_p)}^8.
\]
Combining with \cref{eq:block-three-cs,eq:block-rhs}, and taking eighth roots gives
$|\Lambda|
    \le
    \prod_{j\in J}\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft\lVertL_j\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright\rVert_{U^3}$.
This proves the linear, $1$-bounded case. Applying the initial
reduction proves the general statement.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- definition-6: body_md -->
\label{def:input-model}    
The Reed-Solomon code $C$ is passed in via inputs
$(p,k,\alpha_1,\ldots,\alpha_n)$. We also give as input an integer $\rho p\in[p]$ satisfying $|S_i|\ge \rho p$ for every $i$.
The lists $S_i$ accessed through the coherent membership oracles: for $i\in[n], x\in\mathbb{F}_p, b\in\{0, 1\}$,
\[
    \ket{i}\ket{x}\ket{b}
    \longmapsto
    \ket{i}\ket{x}\ket{b\oplus\mathbf{1}_{S_i}(x)}
\]
The \emph{efficient} classical and quantum algorithms we consider here have running time $\operatorname{poly}(n, \log p)$, which is polynomial whenever $\log p \le \operatorname{poly}(n)$.
When $p\le \operatorname{poly}(n)$, explicit membership truth tables $\{\mathbf{1}_{S_i}(x):i\in [n], x\in \mathbb{F}_p\}$ suffice,
since look-ups are in polynomial time. When $p\ge n^{\omega(1)}$ but $\log p \le \operatorname{poly}(n)$, this is not efficient, so the lists are supplied through membership
oracles above.
<!-- end definition-6 -->
<!-- proposition-1: body_md -->
\label{prop:subsample}
Let $C\subset\mathbb{F}_p^n$, let $S_i\subset\mathbb{F}_p$ with density $\rho_i$, and let
$1\le t\le\min_i|S_i|$.  If the $T_i$ are independent uniformly random
$t$-subsets of $S_i$, then
\[
\mathbb{E}_T\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left[\frac{|C\cap(T_1\times\cdots\times T_n)}
{|C|\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left({t}/{p}\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)^n}\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right]
=
\frac{|C\cap(S_1\times\cdots\times S_n)|}
{|C|\prod_i\rho_i}.
\]
Consequently, a uniform relative-discrepancy bound for input lists of size $t$ transfers without loss to every instance with $|S_i|\ge t$.
<!-- end proposition-1 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
For every fixed
$c\in C\cap(S_1\times\cdots\times S_n)$, independence gives
\[
\mathbb{P}_T(c\in T_1\times\cdots\times T_n)
=\prod_{i=1}^n\frac{t}{|S_i|}.
\]
Summing this identity over $c$ and dividing by $|C|(t/p)^n$ proves the
displayed formula. The second bound follows by averaging.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- proposition-2: body_md -->
\label{prop:oracle-thinning}
Fix $\rho \in (0, 1)$ and $s\in[0,1]$. Suppose a $\operatorname{poly}(n,\log p)$ time quantum algorithm with coherent membership access achieves a satisfaction ratio at least $s$
with inverse-polynomial probability on every OPI instance with density
$\rho_i=\rho$.  Then there is a $\operatorname{poly}(n,\log p,\rho^{-1})$ time quantum algorithm in the same oracle model that achieves the same satisfaction ratio $s$ with high probability whenever $\rho_i\ge\rho$.
<!-- end proposition-2 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
Let $A$ be the given algorithm on fixed density $\rho$, so it has at least inverse-polynomial success probability  $\sigma\in(0,1]$ and polynomial runtime with a polynomial $Q$ many queries of the membership oracles for the input lists when they have size exactly $\rho p$ each. We impose these same time and query bounds on every execution, aborting if they are exceeded, so that $A$ is also defined on inputs outside its density promise. Let 
\[
p_0 := \max\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left\{\frac{16}{\delta}, \frac{32n}{\sigma\delta^2}\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right\}\quad\text{where}\quad \delta:=\frac{\rho\sigma^2}{64Q^2}.
\]
If $p\le p_0$, then $p=\operatorname{poly}(n)$, so we can simply compute the truth table $(\mathbf{1}\{a\in S_i\})_{i\in [n], a\in\mathbb{F}_p}$ with polynomially many queries, arbitrarily throw out elements $S_i$ to obtain $U_i$ with $|U_i|=\rho p$, and run $A$ on $(U_1, \dots, U_n)$. Hence, assume $p\ge p_0$ for the rest of the proof.

First, we efficiently implement membership oracles for
subsets $T_i\subset S_i$ such that for every $i\in [n]$:
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:thinning-size-window}
    \rho\le\frac{|T_i|}{p}\le\rho+\delta.
\end{equation}
For each coordinate $i$, independently draw
\[
    M:=
    \mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left\lceil
      \frac{128}{\delta^2}
      \log\frac{16n}{\sigma}
    \aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right\rceil
\]
uniform field elements $X_{i,1},\ldots,X_{i,M}$, and query
their memberships in $S_i$. Define
\[
    \widetilde\rho_i
    :=
    \frac1M\sum_{\ell=1}^M
       \mathbf1\{X_{i,\ell}\in S_i\}\quad\text{and}\quad \widehat\rho_i := \max(\rho,  \widetilde\rho_i).
\]
For fixed $i$, since $\rho_i\in[\rho,1]$, clipping cannot
increase the estimation error. By Chernoff's inequality
\[
    \mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(
       |\widehat\rho_i-\rho_i|>\frac{\delta}{16}
    \aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)\le \mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(
       |\widetilde\rho_i-\rho_i|>\frac{\delta}{16}
    \aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)
\le
    2\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(
       -2M\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(\frac{\delta}{16}\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)^2
    \aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)\le
    2\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(-\log\frac{16n}{\sigma}\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)
     =\frac{\sigma}{8n}.
\]
Union bounding over $i$, the event $\mathcal{G}$ that $|\widehat\rho_i -\rho_i|\le \delta/16$ occurs with probability at least $1-\sigma/8$. Let
\[
    \theta_i:=
    \min\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left\{1,\frac{\rho+\delta/2}{\widehat\rho_i}\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right\}\quad.
\]
Independently sample uniform $a_i,b_i\in\mathbb F_p$ and define $T_i\subset S_i$ via
\[
    T_i:=
    \mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left\{
       x\in S_i:
  a_ix+b_i\in \{0, \dots, h_i-1\}
    \aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right\}\quad\text{where}\quad     h_i:=\lfloor p\theta_i\rfloor.
\]
On event $\mathcal{G}$, we claim that
\eqref{eq:thinning-size-window} holds with high probability.
If $\theta_i=1$, then $\widehat{\rho_i}\le \rho+\delta/2 $ and $T_i=S_i$, so
\[
    \rho\le\frac{|T_i|}{p}=\rho_i
    \le\widehat\rho_i+\frac{\delta}{16}
    \le\rho+\frac{9\delta}{16}
    <\rho+\delta,
\]
as claimed. If $\theta_i<1$, then let $Z_i=|T_i|/p$. Since $a_ix+b_i$ is uniform on
$\mathbb F_p$ for each $x$, $\mathbb{E}[Z_i]=\rho_i h_i/p$.
Since $ (a_i,b_i)\mapsto(a_ix+b_i, a_iy+b_i)$
is a bijection of $\mathbb F_p^2$ for distinct $x,y$,
\[
    \operatorname{Var}(Z_i)\le \frac{|S_i|}{4p^2}
     \le\frac1{4p}.
\]

Since $h_i=\lfloor p\theta_i\rfloor$
and $\theta_i\widehat\rho_i=\rho+\delta/2$, 
\[
    \mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left|\mathbb{E}[Z_i]-\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(\rho+\frac{\delta}{2}\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right| = \mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left|\frac{\rho_i h_i}{p}-\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(\rho+\frac{\delta}{2}\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right|
    \le \theta_i|\rho_i-\widehat\rho_i|+\frac1p
    \le\frac{\delta}{8},
\]
where the last step uses $p\ge p_0$.
Hence, on event $\mathcal{G}$, Chebyshev gives
\[
    \mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(Z_i\not\in[\rho,\rho+\delta]\middle| \mathcal{G}\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)
    \le
    \mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left|Z_i-\frac{\rho_i h_i}{p}\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right|
       >\frac{\delta}{4}\middle| \mathcal{G}\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)
    \le\frac4{p\delta^2}.
\]
Union bounding over $i\in [n]$ and combining with $p\ge p_0$ and the bound on $\mathcal{G}$, we see that
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:thinning-good-prob}
    \mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(
      \frac{|T_i|}{p}\in[\rho,\rho+\delta]\,\forall\, i\in [n]
    \aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)
    \ge
    1-\frac{\sigma}{4}.
\end{equation}

Note that algorithmically, we never enumerate $T_i$. We simply sample and store $(a_i, b_i, h_i)$. To simulate membership oracles for $T_i$, we query that for $S_i$, compute $\mathbf{1}\{(a_ix+b_i)\in \{0, \dots, h_i\}\}$, and compute the AND of the two bits coherently. This cost polynomial time overhead and allows us to run the algorithm $A$ on input lists $(T_1, \dots T_n)$.
On the high probability event in \cref{eq:thinning-good-prob}, these $T_i$ to have density in $[\rho, \rho+\delta]$. However, they are not yet all equal to $\rho$, so we cannot yet apply the guarantee of satisfaction ratio at least $s$ with probability at least $\sigma$. Nevertheless, we run $A$ on $T_i$ and we show for the rest of the proof why the output has satisfaction ratio at least $s$ with probability at least $\sigma/2$, upon which we boost to high probability with polynomially many repetitions since $\sigma$ is at least inverse polynomial.


Fix any $(T_1, \dots, T_n)$ and condition on the high probability event in \cref{eq:thinning-good-prob}. For analysis only, independently choose $U_i$ uniformly
among the $\rho p$-element subsets of $T_i$.
For every $(i,x)$,
\[
    \mathbb{P}_U\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(
      \mathbf{1}_{T_i}(x)\ne\mathbf{1}_{U_i}(x)
    \aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)
    =
\frac{|T_i|-\rho p}{|T_i|}  \mathbf{1}_{T_i}(x) \le\frac{\delta p}{\rho p}
     =\frac{\delta}{\rho}.
\]
We argue that running $A$ with corresponding membership oracles $O_T$ and $O_U$ produce close-by quantum states.
Write its state just before query $j$ in the execution using $T$
as
\[
    \ket{\psi_j^T}
    =
    \sum_{i,x}\ket{i,x}\ket{\omega_{j,i,x}},
    \qquad
    \sum_{i,x}\|\omega_{j,i,x}\|^2=1.
\]
Let $\Delta:= \{(i, x): \mathbf{1}_{T_i}(x)\ne \mathbf{1}_{U_i}(x)\}$ 
and let $\Pi_{\Delta}$ project onto $\operatorname{span}\{\ket{i,x}:(i,x)\in \Delta\}$ which depends on the random $U$, so
\[
\mathbb{E}_U
       \|\Pi_{\Delta}\ket{\psi_j^T}\|^2
    =
    \sum_{i,x}\|\omega_{j,i,x}\|^2
       \mathbb{P}_U\bigl((i,x)\in\Delta\bigr)
    \le
    \frac{\delta}{\rho}
    \sum_{i,x}\|\omega_{j,i,x}\|^2
     =\frac{\delta}{\rho}.
\]
Now, let us consider applying queries $O_T$ and $O_U$ to state $\ket{\psi_j^T}$, the outcome states overlap outside $\Delta$, and on $\Delta$ the difference $O_T-O_U$ has operator norm at most two, so by Cauchy-Schwarz,
\[
    \mathbb{E}_U
       \|(O_T-O_U)\ket{\psi_j^T}\|\le \mathbb{E}_U \|\Pi_{\Delta_U}\ket{\psi_j^T}\|
    \le
    2\sqrt{
       \mathbb{E}_U
       \|\Pi_{\Delta_U}\ket{\psi_j^T}\|^2
    }
    \le 2\sqrt{\frac{\delta}{\rho}}.
\]

Coupling the two executions, of $A$ with $O_U$ and $O_T$, we bound the error by telescope summing $j$ over the at most $Q$ queries, so the final outcome states $|\Psi_T\rangle,|\Psi_U\rangle$ satisfy
\[
    \mathbb{E}_U
       \bigl\||\Psi_T\rangle-|\Psi_U\rangle\bigr\|
    \le2Q\sqrt{\frac{\delta}{\rho}} = \frac{\sigma}{4},
\]
Every tuple $U$ has exact density $\rho$ and  $U_i\subset S_i$. By the hypothesis on $A$, the outcome of measuring $\ket{\Psi_U}$ has satisfaction ratio at least $s$ with probability at least $\sigma$ for every $U$, since it holds under the fixed event that the output satisfies at least $s$ of the original constraints $S$. Since probability changes by at most the distance between the two output states, the above display shows that measuring $\ket{\Psi_T}$ has satisfaction ratio at least $s$ with probability at least $3\sigma/4$, conditioned on the event in \cref{eq:thinning-good-prob}. Together, we succeed on $S$ with probability
\[
    \mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(1-\frac{\sigma}{4}\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)\frac{3\sigma}{4}
    =
    \frac{3\sigma}{4}-\frac{3\sigma^2}{16}
    \ge\frac{9\sigma}{16},
\]

Now, we amplify this to high probability by repetition, evaluating each output's satisfaction on the original lists $S_i$ with $n$ membership queries and returning the best output. In total, the running time and query complexity overhead is polynomial in $(n,\log p,\rho^{-1},\sigma^{-1},Q)$. Since $\sigma^{-1}$ and $Q$ are polynomial in $n$ and $\log p$, we proved the desired proposition. In particular, $U$ is a proof device, not part of the algorithm.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- remark-4: body_md -->
\label{rem:no-need-rho}
Treating the list size lower bound $\rho p$ as a known input in \cref{def:input-model} is only a convenient convention.
If it is unknown, use $\tilde{O}(\delta^{-2})$ membership queries per coordinate to estimate every $\rho_i=|S_i|/p$ by $\widehat{\rho_i}$ to additive error $\delta$ as in the proof of \cref{prop:oracle-thinning}. Then, apply \cref{prop:oracle-thinning} with
$\underline\rho = \min_i \widehat{\rho_i}-\delta$ suffices with high probability. This preprocessing costs
polynomial overhead and our applications have
arbitrarily small constant slack in the density parameter, so choosing
$\delta$ sufficiently small absorbs the resulting change into the stated
accuracy or strict parameter gap. Thus the density lower bound $\rho$ need not
actually be supplied.
<!-- end remark-4 -->
<!-- lemma-6: body_md -->
\label{lem:translated-opi-proxy}
If $S_i$ has density $\rho_i$ and $v\in\mathbb{F}_p^n$,  then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:translated-EO}
\mathsf{F}_{\operatorname{OPI}}(v)=\mathsf{F}_{\operatorname{OPI}}(v;C,\mathbf{S}):=\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left|
\frac{|(v+C)\cap \mathbf{S}|}
{|C|\prod_i\rho_i}-1
\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right|=\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left|
\sum_{0\ne y\in C^\perp}
e_p(-\langle v,y\rangle)
\prod_{i\in\operatorname{supp}(y)}
\lambda_i\widehat g_i(y_i)
\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right|.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-6 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
Fourier inversion and orthogonality on the coset $v+C$ give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:l31}
\frac{|(v+C)\cap \mathbf{S}|}{|C|}
=
\sum_{y\in C^\perp}
e_p(-\langle v,y\rangle)
\prod_{i=1}^n\widehat{\mathbf{1}_{S_i}}(y_i).
\end{equation}
Now, $\widehat{\mathbf{1}_{S_i}}(0)=\rho_i$, and for $\xi\ne0$,
we know by \cref{eq:centered-local-functions} that
\[ \widehat{\mathbf{1}_{S_i}}(\xi)
=
\sqrt{\rho_i(1-\rho_i)}\widehat{g_i}(\xi).\]
The $y=0$ term in \cref{eq:l31} is $\prod_i\rho_i$.  Subtracting this term and dividing
by $\prod_i\rho_i$ proves \cref{eq:translated-EO}.
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- lemma-7: body_md -->
\label{lem:restated-llr-proxy}
For every $v,v'\in\mathbb{F}_p^n$, we have
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:llr-TwA-form}
d_{\mathrm{TV}}(L_v,L_{v'})^2\le\mathsf{F}_{\operatorname{LLR}}(v,v')\le\mathsf{F}_{\operatorname{LLR}}^+.
\end{equation}
In particular,
$d_{\mathrm{TV}}(L_{s\mathbf{1}},L_{s'\mathbf{1}})
\le\sqrt{\mathsf{F}_{\operatorname{LLR}}(s\mathbf{1},s'\mathbf{1})}\le\sqrt{\mathsf{F}_{\operatorname{LLR}}^+}$.
<!-- end lemma-7 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
For every $x\in\mathbb{F}_p^n$ and $z\in\{0,1\}^n$, we can check the identity
\[
\frac{\mathbf{1}\{\ell_i(x_i)=z_i\}}{\nu_i(z_i)}
=
1+\psi_i(z_i)g_i(x_i),
\]
for every $i\in[n]$.
Consequently,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:llr-likelihood-expansion}
\frac{\mathbb{P}(L_v=z)}{\nu(z)}=
\mathbb{E}_{x\in v+C}\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left[
\prod_{i=1}^n
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(1+\psi_i(z_i)g_i(x_i)\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right]=
\sum_{A\subset[n]}
\psi_A(z)
\mathbb{E}_{x\in v+C}\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left[\prod_{i\in A}g_i(x_i)\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right].
\end{equation}
Applying Fourier inversion on $v + C$, we see that
                           
                                                                 
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:llr-code-moment-expansion}
\mathbb{E}_{x\in v+C}\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left[\prod_{i\in A}g_i(x_i)\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right]=
\sum_{\substack{y\in C^\perp\\\operatorname{supp}(y)=A}}
e_p\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(-\langle v,y\rangle\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)
\prod_{i\in A}\widehat g_i(y_i),
\end{equation}
where above we have used the fact that $\widehat g_i(0) = 0$ for all $i$.
Now, subtracting \cref{eq:llr-likelihood-expansion} for $v$ and $v'$ and
using \cref{eq:llr-code-moment-expansion} gives
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:llr-proxy-key}
\frac{\mathbb{P}(L_v=z)-\mathbb{P}(L_{v'}=z)}{\nu(z)}
=
\sum_{A\subset[n]}
\psi_A(z)
\sum_{\substack{y\in C^\perp\\\operatorname{supp}(y)=A}}
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left[e_p(-\langle v,y\rangle)-e_p(-\langle v',y\rangle)\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right]
\prod_{i\in A}\widehat g_i(y_i).
\end{equation}
Therefore,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ortho-llr-proxy}
\begin{aligned}
4d_{\mathrm{TV}}(L_v,L_{v'})^2
&=
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(
\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim\nu}
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left|
\frac{\mathbb{P}(L_v=Z)-\mathbb{P}(L_{v'}=Z)}{\nu(Z)}
\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right|
\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)^2\\
&\le
\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim\nu}
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left|
\frac{\mathbb{P}(L_v=Z)-\mathbb{P}(L_{v'}=Z)}{\nu(Z)}
\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right|^2\\
&=
\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim\nu}\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left|
\sum_{A\subset[n]}\psi_A(Z)
\sum_{\substack{y\in C^\perp\\\operatorname{supp}(y)=A}}
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left[e_p(-\langle v,y\rangle)-e_p(-\langle v',y\rangle)\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right]
\prod_{i\in A}\widehat g_i(y_i)
\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right|^2\\
&=
4\mathsf{F}_{\operatorname{LLR}}(v, v')
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where we use Cauchy--Schwarz and orthonormality of $\psi_A$. The empty-support contribution cancels. The bound $\mathsf{F}_{\operatorname{LLR}}(v,v')\le\mathsf{F}_{\operatorname{LLR}}^+$ follows from
the triangle inequality.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- theorem-9: body_md -->
\label{thm:kk-black-box}
Let $C\subset\mathbb{F}_p^n$ be any generalized Reed-Solomon code of dimension $k$,
and assume
$k+1\ge n/2$.  Fix $\gamma\in(0,1)$ and for $i \in [n]$, let $u_i:\mathbb{F}_p\to\mathbb{R}$ satisfy
\[
        \mathbb{E} u_i=0,
        \qquad
        \|u_i\|_2\le1,
        \qquad
        \max_{\xi\ne0}|\widehat u_i(\xi)|\le \gamma.
\]
Then, for every $\omega\ne0$, we have that
\[
   \sum_{A\subset[n]}
   \mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft|\sum_{\substack{y\in C^\perp\\\operatorname{supp}(y)=A\\\langle y,\mathbf{1}\rangle=\omega}}
   \prod_{i\in A}\widehat u_i(y_i)\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright|^2
   \le
   \gamma^{2(k+1)}(1+\gamma^{-2})^{n-k-1}.
\]
<!-- end theorem-9 -->
<!-- corollary-2: body_md -->
\label{cor:bias-aware-kk}
Fix $\tau\in[1/2,1)$ and assume that $p>n$ is an odd prime and
$\log p\ll n$. Suppose that $\rho_i\ge\tau$ for all $i$, and define
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:bias-aware-kk-rate}
R_{\mathrm{KK}}(\tau)
:=
\frac{\log(1+\gamma(\tau)^{-2})}
{\log(1+\gamma(\tau)^{-2})-2\log\gamma(\tau)}.
\end{equation}
Every Shamir scheme of fixed rate $R>R_{\mathrm{KK}}(\tau)$ is one-bit locally
leakage resilient against every such leakage tuple.
<!-- end corollary-2 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
For the centered functions $g_i$, using that $\gamma$ is decreasing on
$[1/2,1)$, we have
\[
    \mathbb{E} g_i=0,\qquad \mathbb{E}|g_i|^2=1,
    \qquad
    \max_{\xi\ne0}|\widehat g_i(\xi)|
    \le \gamma(\rho_i)+O(p^{-2})
    \le \gamma_n:=\gamma(\tau)+O(p^{-2})<1.
\]
Since $R>R_{\mathrm{KK}}(\tau)>1/2$,
\cref{thm:kk-black-box} holds for sufficiently large $n$.
Partitioning \cref{eq:FL} according to
$\omega=\langle y,\mathbf{1}\rangle$, the $\omega=0$ contribution vanishes,
and Cauchy--Schwarz gives
{\small \[
    \mathsf{F}_{\operatorname{LLR}}(s\mathbf{1},s'\mathbf{1};C,\mathbf S)
    =
    \frac14\sum_A
    \mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left|
      \sum_{\omega\ne0}
      \bigl(e_p(-s\omega)-e_p(-s'\omega)\bigr)
     \sum_{\substack{y\in C^\perp\\
                    \operatorname{supp}(y)=A\\
                    \langle y,\mathbf{1}\rangle=\omega}}
    \prod_{i\in A}\widehat g_i(y_i)
    \aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right|^2\le
    (p-1)\sum_{\omega\ne0}\sum_A \mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left|\sum_{\substack{y\in C^\perp\\
                    \operatorname{supp}(y)=A\\
                    \langle y,\mathbf{1}\rangle=\omega}}
    \prod_{i\in A}\widehat g_i(y_i)\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right|^2.\]}
Applying
\cref{thm:kk-black-box} separately for each $\omega\ne0$ and then summing
over the $p-1$ frequencies gives
\[
      \mathsf{F}_{\operatorname{LLR}}(s\mathbf{1},s'\mathbf{1};C,\mathbf S)  \le
    (p-1)^2\gamma_n^{2(k+1)}
    \bigl(1+\gamma_n^{-2}\bigr)^{n-k-1} \le  e^{-\Omega(n)}
\]
uniformly over $s,s'\in\mathbb{F}_p$. Note that the rate in the exponent is strictly negative precisely when $R>R_{\mathrm{KK}}(\tau)$, with the fixed gap absorbing the
$o(1)$ error terms.
Finally, \cref{lem:restated-llr-proxy} and the above displayed equation shows $ d_{\mathrm{TV}}(L_{s\mathbf{1}},L_{s'\mathbf{1}})$ is exponentially small,
proving one-bit LLR.
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- remark-5: body_md -->
\label{rem:bias-aware-kk}
At $\tau=1/2$, \cref{eq:bias-aware-kk-rate} is exactly the threshold
$R_{\mathrm{KK}}(1/2)=0.579\ldots$ 
that was established by \cite{kk23} for balanced leakage functions.
The bias-aware centering in 
\cref{eq:FL}
removes the need for a reduction to pass from the balanced to the unbalanced case,
and \cref{fig:R-KK-benchmark} demonstrates the resulting improvement in the unbalanced case.
<!-- end remark-5 -->
<!-- theorem-10: body_md -->
\label{thm:opi-harder}
For any linear code $C\subset \mathbb{F}_p^n$ and a one-bit leakage tuple $(\ell_1,\ldots,\ell_n)$, let $\mathbf{S}:=\ell_1^{-1}(1)\times\dots \times \ell_n^{-1}(1)$ and $\mathbf{S}_z=\ell_1^{-1}(z_1)\times\dots\times \ell_n^{-1}(z_n)$ for $z\in\{0, 1\}^n$. Then,

\begin{enumerate}
    \item Suppose that, for all $s \in \mathbb{F}_p$, and for all $z \in \{0,1\}^n$, $s\mathbf{1} + C$ has near-optimal OPI discrepancy for input lists $S_i = \ell_i^{-1}(z_i)$.  Then $C$ is $1$-bit LLR.
    \item Suppose that, for all $v \in \mathbb{F}_p^n$, and for all $z \in \{0,1\}^n$,  $v+C$ has  near-optimal OPI discrepancy for input lists $S_i = \ell_i^{-1}(z_i)$. Then $d_{\mathrm{TV}}(L_v,L_{v'})\le e^{-\Omega(n)}$ uniformly over $v,v'\in\mathbb{F}_p^n$.
\item At the level of the Fourier proxies and the phase-forgotten Fourier proxies,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:proxy-reductions}
\begin{aligned}
\sqrt{\mathsf{F}_{\operatorname{LLR}}(v,v'; C, \mathbf{S})}
& \le\max_{\substack{u\in\{v,v'\}\\z\in\{0, 1\}^n}}
\mathsf{F}_{\operatorname{OPI}}(u;C,\mathbf{S}_z)\\
\sqrt{\mathsf{F}_{\operatorname{LLR}}^+( C, \mathbf{S})} &
\le\max_{z\in\{0, 1\}^n}\mathsf{F}_{\operatorname{OPI}}^+(C, \mathbf{S}_z).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
                                                                                                                                                                                                         
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end theorem-10 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
We first recall the product Bernoulli distribution $\nu$ as well as the orthonormal basis $\{\psi_A:A\subset [n]\}$ of $L^2(\nu)$ from \cref{eq:prod-ber,eq:bernoulli-basis}.
Then to prove (2), note that
$\mathsf{F}_{\operatorname{OPI}}(v;C,\mathbf S_z)
=\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft|\frac{\mathbb{P}(L_v=z)}{\nu(z)}-1\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright|$ for $z\in\operatorname{supp}(\nu)$.
By the triangle inequality,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:opi-harder-1}
d_{\mathrm{TV}}(L_v,L_{v'})
\le\frac12\sum_{z\in\operatorname{supp}(\nu)}\nu(z)
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(\mathsf{F}_{\operatorname{OPI}}(v;C,\mathbf S_z)+
\mathsf{F}_{\operatorname{OPI}}(v';C,\mathbf S_z)\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)\le
\max_{\substack{u\in\{v,v'\}\\z\in\operatorname{supp}(\nu)}}
\mathsf{F}_{\operatorname{OPI}}(u;C,\mathbf S_z).
\end{equation}
The proof of (1) is similar, replacing $v,v'$ with $s\mathbf{1}$, $s'\mathbf{1}$ for $s \neq s' \in \mathbb{F}_p$.

 To prove (3), recall from \cref{eq:ortho-llr-proxy} that 
\[
\mathsf{F}_{\operatorname{LLR}}(v,v')=\frac14\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim\nu}
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft|\frac{\mathbb{P}(L_v=Z)-\mathbb{P}(L_{v'}=Z)}{\nu(Z)}\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright|^2\le\frac12\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim\nu}
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(\mathsf{F}_{\operatorname{OPI}}(v;C,\mathbf S_Z)^2+
\mathsf{F}_{\operatorname{OPI}}(v';C,\mathbf S_Z)^2\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right),
\]
so the first inequality of \cref{eq:proxy-reductions} follows by bounding the expectations with the maximum. For the second inequality, observe that by definition of $\mathsf{F}_{\operatorname{OPI}}^+(C;\mathbf{S}_z)$ and $\psi_i$, we have that the positive OPI proxy for $\mathbf S_z$ is
\[
\mathsf{F}_{\operatorname{OPI}}^+(C,\mathbf S_z)
=
\sum_{\emptyset\ne A\subset[n]}
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(\prod_{i\in A}|\psi_i(z_i)|\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)
\sum_{\substack{y\in C^\perp\\\operatorname{supp}(y)=A}}
\prod_{i\in A}\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft|\widehat g_i(y_i)\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright|.
\]
Choose $z^\star\in\operatorname{supp}(\nu)$ coordinatewise so that
$|\psi_i(z_i^\star)|=\max\{\lambda_i,\lambda_i^{-1}\}\ge1$.
For this instance $\mathbf{S}_{z^\star}$,
\[
\mathsf{F}_{\operatorname{OPI}}^+(C,\mathbf S_{z^\star})
\ge
\sum_{\emptyset\ne A\subset[n]}
\sum_{\substack{y\in C^\perp\\\operatorname{supp}(y)=A}}
\prod_{i\in A}\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft|\widehat g_i(y_i)\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright|
\ge
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(
\sum_{\emptyset\ne A\subset[n]}
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(
\sum_{\substack{y\in C^\perp\\\operatorname{supp}(y)=A}}
\prod_{i\in A}\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft|\widehat g_i(y_i)\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright|
\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)^2
\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)^{1/2}
=\sqrt{\mathsf{F}_{\operatorname{LLR}}^+(C;\mathbf S)}.
\]
this proves the second inequality of \cref{eq:proxy-reductions} upon observing $\mathsf{F}_{\operatorname{OPI}}^+(C,\mathbf S_{z^\star})\le \max_z \mathsf{F}_{\operatorname{OPI}}^+(C,\mathbf S_{z})$.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- example-1: body_md -->
\label{ex:opi-llr-strict}
Let
$C\subset\mathbb{F}_p^n$ be an $[n,k]$ MDS code with $k=Rn$, where
$R\in(0,1)$ is fixed and $p\ge n$, and corresponding input lists and leakage functions via $ S_i:=\mathbb{F}_p^\times$ and $\ell_i(a):=\mathbf 1\{a\ne0\}$.
For $z\in\{0,1\}^n$, uniformly over $v,v'\in\mathbb{F}_p^n$, 
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:strict-1} 
|\mathbb{P}(L_v=z)-\mathbb{P}(L_{v'}=z)|\le 2^{n+1} p^{-k}
\end{equation}
To see this, write
\[ \mathbf{1}\{L_v=z\} = \prod_{i:z_i=0}\mathbf{1}\{(L_v)_i=0\}\prod_{i:z_i\ne 0}\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(1-\mathbf{1}\{(L_v)_i=0\}\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right) = \sum_{T} (-1)^{|T\cap \operatorname{supp}z|} \mathbf{1}\{L_v\vert_T=0\},\]
summed over all $T\subset [n]$ that contains $\{i:z_i=0\}$. Now, $L_v\vert_T=0$ imposes $|T|$ zeros on coordinates of $v+c$ for $c\sim\operatorname{Unif}(C)$. If $|T|\le k$, $\mathbb{P}(L_v\vert_T=0)$ is independent of $v$ by the MDS property of $C$, so the contributions to \cref{eq:strict-1} cancels for $v$ and $v'$. If $|T|>k$, there is at most one $c\in C$ with the prescribed zeros, so $\mathbb{P}(L_v\vert_T=0)\le p^{-k}$. There are at most $2^{n}$ such $T$, which gives the bound in \cref{eq:strict-1}. 
Hence, we can crudely bound $d_{\mathrm{TV}}(L_v,L_{v'})\le 2^{2n}p^{-k}$.
Moreover, since $\nu(z)\ge p^{-n}$ for every transcript $z$,
\[
    \mathsf{F}_{\operatorname{LLR}}(v,v';C,\mathbf S)
   \le
    \frac{p^n}{4}
    \mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(\sum_z|\mathbb{P}(L_v=z)-\mathbb{P}(L_{v'}=z)|\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)^2
    =
    p^nd_{\mathrm{TV}}(L_v,L_{v'})^2.
\]
Therefore, $\mathsf{F}_{\operatorname{LLR}}(v,v';C,\mathbf S)= e^{-\Omega(n\log n)}$ by \cref{lem:restated-llr-proxy},
whenever $k=Rn$, $R>1/2$, and $p\ge n$.
Nevertheless, for $z=0^n$ we have $S_z=\{0\}^n$ and
$C\cap S_z=\{0\}$, so
\[
    \mathsf{F}_{\operatorname{OPI}}(0;C,S_z)
    =
    \mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left|\frac{p^{-k}}{p^{-n}}-1\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right|
    =
    p^{n-k}-1.
\]
Thus both $d_{\mathrm{TV}}(L_v,L_{v'})$ and the LLR proxy can be
superexponentially small even though the worst-case OPI
discrepancy is superexponentially large.
<!-- end example-1 -->
<!-- lemma-8: body_md -->
\label{lem:mpsw-fixed-support}
Fix $b\in\mathbb{N}$. There is a constant $C_b>1$ such that the following
holds. Let $C\subset\mathbb{F}_p^n$ be the secret-zero share code of a Shamir scheme of rate $R$,
and choose $0<\sigma\le 2R-1$, $a>1$, and positive integers $T$ and $\ell=2^bT^2$.
If $C^\perp$ passes $\operatorname{TEST}_{C^\perp}(\sigma,\ell,a)$, then the
scheme is $b$-bit locally leakage resilient with leakage error
\[
\varepsilon\le 
2^{-[R\log_2 C_b-\log_2 a-H_2(\sigma)]n}+2^{-[\sigma\log_2 T-(\sigma+1-R)b-H_2(\sigma)]n}.
\]
<!-- end lemma-8 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
Fix the constant $C_b$ from \cref{lem:mpsw-fixed-support}. First choose
$0<\sigma<2R-1$ so that
$H_2(\sigma)<R\log_2 C_b$. Next choose $a>1$ sufficiently close to
one that
\[
R\log_2 C_b-\log_2a-H_2(\sigma)>0,
\]
and finally choose $T$ sufficiently large that
\[
\sigma\log_2T-(\sigma+1-R)b-H_2(\sigma)>0.
\]
Put $\ell:=2^bT^2$. Our choice $0<\sigma<2R-1<R$ gives agreement $1-\sigma>1-R$, strictly above the dual rate, so
\cref{thm:rs-lr} implies that $C^\perp$ is
$(\sigma,\ell,L)$-list recoverable with
$L=n^{O_{R,b}(1)}\ll a^n$ as $n\to\infty$.
Hence $C^\perp$ passes
$\operatorname{TEST}_{C^\perp}(\sigma,\ell,a)$, and
the two exponents in the square brackets in \cref{lem:mpsw-fixed-support} are positive
constants. This proves exponentially small leakage error.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
First, we claim that it suffices to show the case of exact norm bounds, i.e. without the uniform
$\varepsilon=o(1)$ error. Scale  $\widetilde w_i:=\lambda^{-1}w_i$ so that $ \|\widetilde w_i\|_\infty\le\gamma$ and $ \|\widetilde w_i\|_2^2\le\Gamma$ for every $i$, via
\[
    \lambda:=
    \max\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left\{
      \frac{\gamma+\varepsilon_n}{\gamma},
      \sqrt{\frac{\Gamma+\varepsilon_n}{\Gamma}}
    \aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right\}
    =1+o(1).
\]
Then, since $|y|\le n$, the result for $w_i$ follows from that of $\widetilde w_i$ as
\[
\sum_{\substack{0\ne y\in C^\perp\\|y|\le t}}
\prod_{i\in\operatorname{supp}(y)}w_i(y_i)
\le
\lambda^n
\sum_{\substack{0\ne y\in C^\perp\\|y|\le t}}
\prod_{i\in\operatorname{supp}(y)}\widetilde w_i(y_i),
\]
and
$\lambda^n=e^{o(n)}$. Therefore assume throughout the proof that $ \|w_i\|_\infty\le\gamma$ and $ \|w_i\|_2^2\le\Gamma$ for every $i$.

Now, If $t\le k$, then the sum is empty by the minimum distance $k+1$ of the dual code $C^\perp$.  
For $t> k$,  by assumption, $\sigma:=\lfloor k-t/2\rfloor /n$ is bounded away from $0$. Choose a constant
$\theta\in(0,\gamma)$ such that
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:quant-llr-small}
3(1+\Gamma) \theta^\sigma\le \gamma.
\end{equation}
For each coordinate $i \in [n]$, define the \emph{heavy list} $H_i
:=
\{0\}\cup
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left\{\xi\ne0:w_i(\xi)>\theta\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right\}$.
Markov's inequality implies
 that $\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft|H_i\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright|\le1+\Gamma\theta^{-2}$.  Since
$\sigma<R-t/(2n) = R-\Omega(1)$, \cref{thm:rs-lr} shows that only
$L=n^{O(1)}$ elements of $C^\perp$ agree with the lists $H_i$ on at least
$(1-\sigma)n$ coordinates.
Write $J(y):=\{i:y_i\in H_i\}$ and split weighted sum as
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:low-high-gi}
\sum_{\substack{0\ne y\in C^\perp\\|y|\le t}}
\prod_{i\in\operatorname{supp}(y)}w_i(y_i)
=
\sum_{\substack{0\ne y\in C^\perp\\|y|\le t\\
|J(y)|\ge(1-\sigma)n}}
\prod_{i\in\operatorname{supp}(y)}w_i(y_i)
\\
+
\sum_{\substack{0\ne y\in C^\perp\\|y|\le t\\
|J(y)|<(1-\sigma)n}}
\prod_{i\in\operatorname{supp}(y)}w_i(y_i).
\end{equation}
We bound the two summands separately.
\begin{itemize}
\item For nonzero $y\in C^\perp$ with $\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft|J(y)\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright|\ge (1-\sigma)n$, as $\sigma < R$, list recovery (\cref{thm:rs-lr}) of $C^\perp$ of distance $k+1$, and the pointwise weight bound give
\[
\sum_{\substack{0\ne y\in C^\perp\\|y|\le t\\
|J(y)|\ge(1-\sigma)n}}
\prod_{i\in\operatorname{supp}(y)}w_i(y_i)
\le
L\gamma^{k+1} = n^{O(1)}\gamma^{k}.
\]

\item For nonzero $y\in C^\perp$ with
$\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft|J(y)\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright|<(1-\sigma)n$, because $0\in H_i$,
every coordinate $i$ outside $J(y)$ belongs to $\operatorname{supp}(y)$.  For any such $i \not\in J(y),$ the weight $w_i(y_i)$ 
 is at most $\theta$ by the definitions of $H_i$ and $J(y)$.
Since $|y|\le t$, choose $T\in\binom{[n]}t$ containing
$\operatorname{supp}(y)$. Counting by $T$ and
the possible sets $M\in\binom{T}{\sigma n}$ of $\sigma n$ coordinates outside $J(y)$
gives
\[
\sum_{\substack{0\ne y\in C^\perp\\
|y|\le t,\ |J(y)|<(1-\sigma)n}}
\prod_{i\in\operatorname{supp}(y)}w_i(y_i)
\le
\sum_{T\in\binom{[n]}t}
\sum_{M\in\binom{T}{\sigma n}}
\sum_{\substack{y\in C^\perp\\
\operatorname{supp}(y)\subset T\\
y_i\notin H_i\ \forall i\in M}}
\prod_{i\in\operatorname{supp}(y)}w_i(y_i).
\]
Fix $T$ and one such $M$.
Since $2k-t>0$, we have
\[
    \sigma n=\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left\lfloor k-\frac t2\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right\rfloor
    \le k-\frac t2
    \le2k-t,
\]
and so $|T\setminus M|=t-\sigma n\ge2(t-k)$.
Hence, $T\setminus M$ contains two
disjoint sets $J_1,J_2$ of size $t-k$.
Extracting the $\theta^{|M|}$ contribution on $M$
and applying Cauchy--Schwarz gives
\begin{align*}
\sum_{\substack{y\in C^\perp\\
\operatorname{supp}(y)\subset T\\
y_i\notin H_i\ \forall i\in M}}
\prod_{i\in\operatorname{supp}(y)}w_i(y_i)
&\le
\theta^{|M|}
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(
\sum_{\substack{y\in C^\perp\\\operatorname{supp}(y)\subset T}}
\prod_{i\in J_1\cap\operatorname{supp}(y)}w_i(y_i)^2
\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)^{1/2}
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(
\sum_{\substack{y\in C^\perp\\\operatorname{supp}(y)\subset T}}
\prod_{i\in J_2\cap\operatorname{supp}(y)}w_i(y_i)^2
\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)^{1/2}
\\
&=
\theta^{\sigma n}
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(
\prod_{i\in J_1}
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left[1+\sum_{a\ne0}w_i(a)^2\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right]
\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)^{1/2}
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(
\prod_{i\in J_2}
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left[1+\sum_{a\ne0}w_i(a)^2\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right]
\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)^{1/2}
\\
&\le
\theta^{\sigma n}(1+\Gamma)^{t-k},
\end{align*}
where the crucial observation is that the shortening of
$C^\perp$ to $T$ is an $[t,t-k]$ MDS code, so projection
onto either $J_1$ or $J_2$ is a bijection.
The number of choices of $(T, M)$ is at most $3^n$ as $M\subset T\subset [n]$, so by our choice \cref{eq:quant-llr-small}, we see that
\[
\sum_{\substack{0\ne y\in C^\perp\\
|y|\le t,\ |J(y)|<(1-\sigma)n}}
\prod_{i\in\operatorname{supp}(y)}w_i(y_i)\le 
3^n
\theta^{\sigma n}(1+\Gamma)^{t-k}
\le
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left[3(1+\Gamma)\theta^\sigma\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right]^n
\le\gamma^n.
\]
\end{itemize}
Combining the two estimates in the bullet points proves the lemma.
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- remark-6: body_md -->
\label{rem:wftl-param}
We remark on the requirement in \cref{lem:wftl} that $2k -t = \Omega(n)$, and its implications.  First, this requirement comes from the fact that
the proof of \cref{lem:wftl} requires $\sigma <R$ in order to apply \cref{thm:rs-lr}, and requires $\sigma n\le2k-t$  so that $|T \setminus M|$ is sufficiently large; the existence of such a $\sigma$ is guaranteed when $2k-t = \Omega(n)$, hence the hypothesis.  

As for why this matters, it means that if we want to apply \cref{lem:wftl} for every $t\in [n]$, including $t=n$---which, for example, is what we do in the proof of a more quantitative statement of~\cref{thm:main-llr}(1) below, and in the proof of \cref{thm:main-opi}(1)---we will then need $2k-n\gtrsim n$, i.e. rate $R>1/2$.  This is one of the two apparent barriers at rate $1/2$ discussed in \cref{sec:intro} that we will need to circumvent for our results with $R < 1/2$.
<!-- end remark-6 -->
<!-- corollary-3: body_md -->
\label{cor:one-bit-above}
Fix $R>1/2$. Let $C$ be an $[n,k]$ GRS code over a prime
field $\mathbb{F}_p$, with $p\ge n$ and $k/n\ge R$.
 Then, uniformly over the leakage functions $\ell_1, \ldots, \ell_n$  and $v,v'\in\mathbb{F}_p^n$,
\[
d_{\mathrm{TV}}(L_v,L_{v'})\le\sqrt{\mathsf{F}_{\operatorname{LLR}}(v,v')}\le\sqrt{\mathsf{F}_{\operatorname{LLR}}^+}
\le n^{O_R(1)}\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(\frac{2}{\pi}\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)^k.
\]
<!-- end corollary-3 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
By \cref{lem:restated-llr-proxy} and the definition of $\mathsf{F}_{\operatorname{LLR}}^+$
\[
d_{\mathrm{TV}}(L_v,L_{v'})
\le\sqrt{\mathsf{F}_{\operatorname{LLR}}(v,v')}\le\sqrt{\mathsf{F}_{\operatorname{LLR}}^+}
\le\sum_{0\ne y\in C^\perp}
\prod_{i\in\operatorname{supp}(y)}|\widehat g_i(y_i)|.
\]
Apply \cref{lem:wftl} to $w_i(\xi)=|\widehat g_i(\xi)|$ with parameters $t=n$, $\Gamma=1$, and
$\gamma=2/\pi+O(p^{-2})$ from \cref{cor:L-infty-bound}.
Since $2k-n\ge(2R-1)n$, we obtain the bound
\[
\sum_{0\ne y\in C^\perp}
\prod_{i\in\operatorname{supp}(y)}|\widehat g_i(y_i)|
\le n^{O_R(1)}\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(\frac{2}{\pi}+O(p^{-2})\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)^k
\le n^{O_R(1)}\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(\frac{2}{\pi}\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)^k.
\]
Combining the two equations above gives the corollary.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- lemma-9: body_md -->
\label{lem:high-support-energy}
There exists an absolute constant $q\in (0, 1)$ such that for every $v\in\mathbb{F}_p^n$, every balanced leakage tuple and its centered functions $g_i$
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:high-support-energy}
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft|
\sum_{\substack{y\in C^\perp\\\operatorname{supp}(y)=A}}
e_p(-\langle v,y\rangle)
\prod_{i\in A}\widehat g_i(y_i)
\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright|
=\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft|\mathbb{E}_{x\in v+C}\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft[\prod_{i\in A}g_i(x_i)\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright]\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright|
\le 2^{o(n)}q^{(3k-n)/2}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-9 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
The first equality is Fourier inversion, since $\widehat g_i(0)=0$.
In the balanced case, $\rho_i\sim 1/2$ so centered $g_i$ has mean zero and takes values $\pm 1+o(1)$. Thus,
\cref{lem:u3-gap} gives constant $q\in (0, 1)$ such that
$\|g_i\|_{U^3}\le q$ 
for every $i$.
Fix any $J\subset A$ of size $d:=\lfloor(3k-n)/2\rfloor$.

We next apply \cref{lem:block-u3}, where the functions $F_i$ map a message $x$ to the $i$-th symbol of its encoding in $v+C$, and where $L_i=g_i$ for $i\in A$ and $L_i=1$ for $i\not\in A$, to obtain
\[
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft|\mathbb{E}_{x\in v+C}\prod_{i\in A}g_i(x_i)\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright|
\le
\prod_{i\in A\setminus J}\|g_i\|_\infty
\prod_{j\in J}\|g_j\|_{U^3}
\le
2^{o(n)}q^d
=
2^{o(n)}q^{(3k-n)/2},
\]
where various $o(1)$ error at each coordinate is absorbed into the $2^{o(n)}$ term.
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- lemma-10: body_md -->
\label{lem:balanced-llr-below}
There exists $\delta>0$ such that for every
$[n,k]$ generalized Reed-Solomon code $C$ of rate $R\ge1/2-\delta$ over a prime
field $\mathbb{F}_p$ with $p\ge n$, every balanced one-bit
leakage tuple, and every $v, v'\in \mathbb{F}_p^n$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:balanced-llr-proxy-below}
\mathsf{F}_{\operatorname{LLR}}(v, v')
\le e^{-\Omega(n)}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-10 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
For $R>1/2$, this is true by \cref{cor:one-bit-above}. We assume $R\in [1/2-\delta, 1/2]$, where we choose $\delta>0$
sufficiently small that
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:llr-below-condition}
H_2\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(\frac52\delta-\delta^2\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)
+\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(\frac12-3\delta\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)\log_2q<0.
\end{equation}
Such a choice exists because the left-hand side is strictly
negative at $\delta=0$.
Use the cutoff $m=\lfloor(2-\delta)k\rfloor$ from
\cref{eq:puncture-size}. Forgetting phases 
and using $\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft|a-b\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright|^2\le2\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft|a\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright|^2+2\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft|b\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright|^2$, we have
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:llr-below-low-high}
\mathsf{F}_{\operatorname{LLR}}(v,v')\le 
\sum_{0<|A|\le m}
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(
\sum_{\substack{y\in C^\perp\\\operatorname{supp}(y)=A}}
\prod_{i\in A}\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft|\widehat g_i(y_i)\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright|
