\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)^2+
\frac12\sum_{u\in\{v,v'\}}\sum_{|A|>m}
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft|
\sum_{\substack{y\in C^\perp\\\operatorname{supp}(y)=A}}
e_p(-\langle u,y\rangle)
\prod_{i\in A}\widehat g_i(y_i)
\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright|^2.
\end{equation}
We bound the two summands separately.
\begin{itemize}
\item For the first term, apply \cref{lem:wftl} with $w_i=|\widehat{g_i}|$,
 $t=m$, and $\gamma =2/\pi$ via \cref{cor:L-infty-bound}, and $\Gamma=1$ via Parseval's identity. We check that $2k-m\sim \delta k\asymp n$, so
\begin{equation}
     \sum_{0<|A|\le m}
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(
\sum_{\substack{y\in C^\perp\\\operatorname{supp}(y)=A}}
\prod_{i\in A}\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft|\widehat g_i(y_i)\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright|
\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)^2\le
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(
\sum_{\substack{0\ne y\in C^\perp\\|y|\le m}}
\prod_{i\in\operatorname{supp}(y)}\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft|\widehat g_i(y_i)\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright|
\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)^2\le
n^{O(1)}\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(\frac{2}{\pi}\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)^{2k}
=e^{-\Omega(n)}.
\end{equation}
\item For the second term, we apply \cref{lem:high-support-energy} to each term and count the number of $A$ via \cref{eq:high-support-count}:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\frac12\sum_{u\in\{v,v'\}}\sum_{|A|>m}
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft|
\sum_{\substack{y\in C^\perp\\\operatorname{supp}(y)=A}}
e_p(-\langle u,y\rangle)
\prod_{i\in A}\widehat g_i(y_i)
\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright|^2 & \le |\{A\subset [n]:|A|>m\}| \cdot 2^{o(n)}q^{3k-n}
\\ & \le 2^{\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left[
H_2\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(\frac52\delta-\delta^2\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)
+\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(\frac12-3\delta\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)\log_2q+o(1)
\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right]n}
\\ & =2^{-\Omega(n)}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where the last inequality uses $R\ge1/2-\delta$, $\log_2q<0$, and \cref{eq:llr-below-condition}.
\end{itemize}
Combining the two estimates proves \cref{lem:balanced-llr-below}.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- remark-7: body_md -->
We remark that balance is used only for the large-support estimate: it makes the centered functions nearly $1$-bounded while retaining a fixed $U^3$ gap. The low-support estimate already holds for arbitrary biases.
<!-- end remark-7 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
Part~(1) is proved in \cref{subsec:llr-above} by combining
\cref{lem:mpsw-fixed-support} with \cref{thm:rs-lr}.
For part~(2), \cref{lem:arbitrary-llr-below} bounds
$d_{\mathrm{TV}}(L_v,L_{v'})$ exponentially for every one-bit leakage tuple.
Taking $v=s\mathbf{1}$ and $v'=s'\mathbf{1}$ proves the theorem.
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- lemma-11: body_md -->
\label{lem:positive-opi-above}
For every $[n,k]$ generalized Reed--Solomon code $C$ of fixed rate
$R>1/2$ over a prime field $\mathbb{F}_p$ with $p\ge n$, and every OPI
instance $\mathbf S$ of fixed density $\rho\in(0,1)$,
\[
\mathsf{F}_{\operatorname{OPI}}^+(C;\mathbf S)
\le n^{O(1)}
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(\frac{\sin(\pi\rho)}{\pi\rho}\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)^k
=e^{-\Omega(n)}.
\]
<!-- end lemma-11 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
By Parseval and \cref{cor:L-infty-bound}, with
$\lambda=\sqrt{(1-\rho)/\rho}$,
\[
\sum_{\xi\ne0}\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(\lambda|\widehat g_i(\xi)|\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)^2
=\frac{1-\rho}{\rho},
\qquad
\lambda|\widehat g_i(\xi)|
\le\frac{\sin(\pi\rho)}{\pi\rho}+O_\rho(p^{-2}).
\]
Since $\sin(\pi\rho)/(\pi\rho)<1$, apply \cref{lem:wftl}
to $w_i(\xi)=\lambda|\widehat g_i(\xi)|$ with $t=n$,
$\Gamma=(1-\rho)/\rho$, and
$\gamma=\sin(\pi\rho)/(\pi\rho)$. We obtain
\[
\mathsf{F}_{\operatorname{OPI}}^+
=\sum_{0\ne y\in C^\perp}
\prod_{i\in\operatorname{supp}(y)}\lambda|\widehat g_i(y_i)|
\le n^{O(1)}
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(\frac{\sin(\pi\rho)}{\pi\rho}\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)^k
=e^{-\Omega(n)}.\qedhere
\]
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- lemma-12: body_md -->
\label{lem:balanced-opi-below}
There exists $\delta>0$ such that for every $[n,k]$
generalized Reed--Solomon code $C$ of fixed rate $R\ge1/2-\delta$
over a prime field $\mathbb{F}_p$ with $p\ge n$, every balanced OPI instance
(that is, where $\rho_i=\rho=1/2+o(1)$),
and every $v\in\mathbb{F}_p^n$,
\[
\mathsf{F}_{\operatorname{OPI}}(v)\le e^{-\Omega(n)}.
\]
<!-- end lemma-12 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
When $R>1/2$, the statement follows from \cref{lem:positive-opi-above}, so we assume $R \leq 1/2$. Otherwise, fix the absolute constant $q<1$ from \cref{lem:high-support-energy}, choose $\delta>0$ sufficiently small, take $R\in[1/2-\delta,1/2]$, and use the same cutoff
$m=\lfloor(2-\delta)k\rfloor$ from \cref{eq:puncture-size}.
Choose $\delta$ so that
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:opi-below-condition}
H_2\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(\frac52\delta-\delta^2\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)
+\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(\frac14-\frac32\delta\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)\log_2q<0.
\end{equation}
Such a choice exists because the left-hand side is strictly negative at $\delta=0$.
Write $\rho_i=\rho=1/2+o(1)$ and $\lambda=\sqrt{(1-\rho)/\rho}$. For every support $A$, $\prod_{i\in A}\lambda_i=\lambda^{|A|}=2^{o(n)}$
uniformly in $A$, so we may ignore the $\lambda$'s. Applying \cref{eq:translated-EO} and taking the triangle inequality over supports gives
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:opi-below-low-high}
\mathsf{F}_{\operatorname{OPI}}(v)\le
2^{o(n)}
\sum_{\substack{0\ne y\in C^\perp\\|y|\le m}}
\prod_{i\in\operatorname{supp}(y)}\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft|\widehat g_i(y_i)\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright|
+2^{o(n)}
\sum_{\substack{A\subset[n]\\|A|>m}}
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft|\sum_{\substack{y\in C^\perp\\\operatorname{supp}(y)=A}}
e_p(-\langle v,y\rangle)\prod_{i\in A}\widehat g_i(y_i)\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright|.
\end{equation}
We bound the two summands separately.
\begin{itemize}
\item For $|y|\le m$, we apply \cref{lem:wftl} exactly as in the small-support part of the proof of \cref{lem:balanced-llr-below}:
\[
\sum_{\substack{0\ne y\in C^\perp\\|y|\le m}}
\prod_{i\in\operatorname{supp}(y)}\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft|\widehat g_i(y_i)\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright|
\le n^{O(1)}\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(\frac2\pi\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)^k=e^{-\Omega(n)}.
\]
\item For $|A|>m$, we apply \cref{lem:high-support-energy} to each term and count supports using \cref{eq:high-support-count}:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\sum_{\substack{A\subset[n]\\|A|>m}}
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft|\sum_{\substack{y\in C^\perp\\\operatorname{supp}(y)=A}}
e_p(-\langle v,y\rangle)\prod_{i\in A}\widehat g_i(y_i)\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright|
&\le
2^{\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left[H_2\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(\frac52\delta-\delta^2\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)+\frac{3R-1}{2}\log_2q+o(1)\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right]n}
\\ &\le
2^{\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left[H_2\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(\frac52\delta-\delta^2\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)+\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(\frac14-\frac32\delta\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)\log_2q+o(1)\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right]n}
\\ & =2^{-\Omega(n)}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The last inequality uses $R\ge1/2-\delta$, $\log_2q<0$,
and \cref{eq:opi-below-condition}.
\end{itemize}
Combining the two estimates proves \cref{lem:balanced-opi-below}upon multiplying by $2^{o(n)}$.
<!-- end proof-18 -->
<!-- lemma-13: body_md -->
\label{lem:key-fourier}
For any $0\le t\le n$ and every $x\in\mathbb{F}_p^n$
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:key-fourier}
\mathbb{E}_{x\in C}[q_t(x)]
=
\sum_{\substack{y\in C^\perp\\|y|=t}}
\prod_{i\in\operatorname{supp}(y)}\widehat g_i(y_i).
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-13 -->
<!-- lemma-14: body_md -->
\label{lem:layer-recursion}
For $0\le j,j'\le n$ and any $x\in \mathbb{F}_p^n$,
\begin{equation}
q_jq_{j'}=\sum_{t=0}^nN_\rho(j,j';t)q_t
\quad\text{and}\quad
q_1q_j =(j+1)q_{j+1}+(n-j+1)q_{j-1}
+\beta_\rho j q_j,
\end{equation}
where $ N_\rho(j,j';t)$ and its upper bound $ \tilde{N_\rho}(j,j';t)\ge |N_\rho(j, j';t)|$ are defined as follows:
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:def-N}
\begin{aligned}
 N_\rho(j,j';t)&:=[X^jY^{j'}]
(1+XY)^{n-t}(X+Y+\beta_\rho XY)^t\\
\tilde{N_\rho}(j,j';t)&:= [X^jY^{j'}]
(1+XY)^{n-t}(X+Y+|\beta_\rho| XY)^t.\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-14 -->
<!-- theorem-11: body_md -->
\label{thm:sw-main}
Fix $\alpha\in (0, 1-\rho]$ and let $\ell =\lfloor \alpha n\rfloor$.
Suppose that, for some fixed $c>0$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:key-decay}
\frac{\tilde N_\rho(j,j';t)}
{\sqrt{\binom nj\binom n{j'}}}
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft|\mathbb{E}_{x\in C}q_t(x)\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright|
\le e^{-cn}
\end{equation}
uniformly for $k<t\le n$ and
$\ell-\sigma-1\le j,j'\le\ell+1$. Then, for some $c'>0$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:sw-main}
\begin{aligned}
\mathbb{E}_{x\in C}\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left[F_\ell(x)^2\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right]
&=\sigma+1+O(e^{-c'n}),\\
\mathbb{E}_{x\in C}[s(x)F_\ell(x)^2]
&=(\sigma+1)\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left[
\mathsf{SCL}_\rho(\alpha)
+O_{\rho,\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(\frac{\sigma}{n}+\frac1\sigma\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)
\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right]+O(e^{-c'n}).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Consequently, measuring the normalized state $\ket{\varphi_\ell}$ gives expected satisfaction ratio $\mathsf{SCL}_\rho(\alpha)+o(1)$.
<!-- end theorem-11 -->
<!-- lemma-15: body_md -->
\label{lem:N-proxy-bound}
If $t\in [n]$ satisfies $2k-t=\Omega(n)$, then
uniformly over the input lists,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:N-proxy-bound}
\sum_{\substack{0\ne y\in C^\perp\\|y|\le t}}
b_\rho^{|y|}
\prod_{i\in\operatorname{supp}(y)}|\widehat g_i(y_i)|
\le e^{-\Omega(n)} \quad\text{where}\quad b_\rho:=\sqrt{\frac {1-\rho}{\rho}} (2\rho+|1-2\rho|)
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-15 -->
<!-- proof-19: body_md -->
We apply \cref{lem:wftl} to
$w_i(\xi):=b_\rho|\widehat g_i(\xi)|$, so we first check the conditions on $w_i$:
\begin{itemize}
\item If $\rho\le1/2$, then
$b_\rho=\sqrt{(1-\rho)/\rho}$. Thus
\cref{lem:indicator-L-infty-bound} gives, for $\xi\ne0$,
\[
w_i(\xi)
=\frac{|\widehat{\mathbf{1}_{S_i}}(\xi)|}{\rho}
\le\frac{\sin(\pi\rho)}{\rho p\sin(\pi/p)}
=\frac{\sin(\pi\rho)}{\pi\rho}+O_\rho(p^{-2}).
\]
The limiting bound is strictly less than one for fixed $\rho\in (0,1)$, uniformly over $\xi\ne 0$.
\item If $\rho\ge1/2$, let $x:=2\rho-1\in[0,1)$. Then $b_\rho\le\sqrt{2}$ as
\[
b_\rho^2
=\frac{1+3x-4x^3}{1+x}=2-\frac{1-x+4x^3}{1+x}\le 2.
\]
By \cref{cor:L-infty-bound},
$w_i(\xi)\le2\sqrt2/\pi+O(p^{-2})<1$
for all sufficiently large $p$.
\end{itemize}
In both cases there is a fixed $\gamma_\rho<1$ bounding all
$w_i(\xi)$ for sufficiently large $n$.
Parseval's identity implies that
\[
\sum_{\xi\ne0}w_i(\xi)^2
=
b_\rho^2\sum_{\xi\ne0}|\widehat g_i(\xi)|^2
=
b_\rho^2\mathbb{E}|g_i|^2
=
b_\rho^2
\le
\max\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left\{\frac{1-\rho}{\rho},2\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right\}=:\Gamma.
\]
If $t\le k$, the sum on the left of \cref{eq:N-proxy-bound} vanishes by the dual distance.
Otherwise, by assumption that $2k-t=\Omega(n)$, \cref{lem:wftl} with $\gamma = \gamma_\rho$ and $\Gamma$ as defined above upper bounds the left hand side of \cref{eq:N-proxy-bound} by $e^{-\Omega(n)}$.
<!-- end proof-19 -->
<!-- lemma-16: body_md -->
\label{lem:key-truncation}
With $H_\ell$ defined in \cref{eq:def-H-ell}, we have that
\begin{equation}
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left|\mathbb{E}_{x\in C}[H_\ell(x)^2]-1\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right|\le e^{-\Omega(n)}\quad\text{and}\quad  0\le\mathbb{E}_{x\in C}[(1-s(x))H_\ell(x)^2]\le e^{-\Omega(n)}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-16 -->
<!-- proof-20: body_md -->
Let $a_j:=\rho^{(n-j)/2}(1-\rho)^{j/2}$ and
$z:=\sqrt{(1-\rho)/\rho}$, so $a_j=\rho^{n/2}z^j$. 
Expanding
$H_\ell^2$ gives
\begin{align*}
\mathbb{E}_{x\in C}[H_\ell(x)^2]
=
\sum_{j,j'=0}^{\ell}a_ja_{j'}
\sum_{t=0}^{n}N_\rho(j,j';t)\mathbb{E}_{x\in C}[q_t(x)]
=
\pi_{\le\ell}
+
\sum_{t=1}^{\min(n,2\ell)}
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(
\sum_{j,j'=0}^{\ell}a_ja_{j'}N_\rho(j,j';t)
\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)\mathbb{E}_{x\in C}[q_t(x)],
\end{align*}
by \cref{lem:layer-recursion}.
The $t=0$ term is $\pi_{\le\ell}$ because
$N_\rho(j,j';0)=\binom nj\mathbf{1}\{j=j'\}$ and $q_0=1$. Now, since $\tilde{N}_\rho$ are nonnegative coefficients, \cref{eq:def-N}  gives for every $1\le t\le \min(n, 2\ell)$ that
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:truncation-proxy}
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left|
\sum_{j,j'=0}^{\ell}a_ja_{j'}N_\rho(j,j';t)
\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right|
\le
\sum_{j,j'=0}^{\ell}a_ja_{j'}\tilde N_\rho(j,j';t)\le
\sum_{j,j'=0}^{n}a_ja_{j'}\tilde N_\rho(j,j';t)=
\rho^n(1+z^2)^{n-t}(2z+|\beta_\rho|z^2)^t.
\end{equation}
We claim that the last quantity is exactly $b_\rho^t$. This follows from $1+z^2=1/\rho$ and from \cref{eq:sw-layer-definition}:
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:b-rho}
   \beta_\rho:=\frac{1-2\rho}{\sqrt{\rho(1-\rho)}} \implies  b_\rho = 2\sqrt{\rho(1-\rho)}+(1-\rho)|\beta_\rho| = \rho(2z+z^2|\beta_\rho|).
\end{equation}
Therefore, by \cref{lem:N-proxy-bound}, we have that
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:key-trunction-2}
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left|\mathbb{E}_{x\in C}[H_\ell(x)^2]-\pi_{\le\ell}\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right|
\le
\sum_{t=1}^{\min(n,2\ell)}
b_\rho^t\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left|\mathbb{E}_{x\in C}[q_t(x)]\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right|
\le
\sum_{\substack{0\ne y\in C^\perp\\|y|\le\min(n,2\ell)}}
b_\rho^{|y|}
\prod_{i\in\operatorname{supp}(y)}|\widehat g_i(y_i)|
\le e^{-\Omega(n)},
\end{equation}
where the last inequality follows from \cref{lem:key-fourier} upon checking that, by \cref{eq:def-ell-alpha}, 
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:truncation-gap}
2k-\min(n,2\ell+1)\ge2(k-\ell)-1=\Omega(n).
\end{equation}
Finally, the first bound in \cref{lem:key-truncation} follows triangle inequality and \cref{eq:truncation-0}:
\[
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left|\mathbb{E}_{x\in C}[H_\ell(x)^2]-1\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right|
\le
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left|\mathbb{E}_{x\in C}[H_\ell(x)^2]-\pi_{\le\ell}\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right|
+(1-\pi_{\le\ell})
\le e^{-\Omega(n)}.
\]

For the second bound, recall \cref{eq:sw-score-ratio}, apply 
\cref{lem:layer-recursion}, and use $a_j=\rho^{(n-j)/2}(1-\rho)^{j/2}$ to expand
\begin{align*}
(1-s)H_\ell
&=
(1-\rho)\sum_{j=0}^{\ell}a_jq_j
-\frac{\sqrt{\rho(1-\rho)}}{n}
\sum_{j=0}^{\ell}a_j
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left[
(j+1)q_{j+1}+(n-j+1)q_{j-1}+\beta_\rho j q_j
\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right]\\
&=
\sum_{j=0}^{\ell}a_j
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left[
1-\rho-
\frac{j\rho+(n-j)(1-\rho)+(1-2\rho)j}{n}
\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right]q_j+
\frac{\sqrt{\rho(1-\rho)}}{n}
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left[
(n-\ell)a_{\ell+1}q_\ell
-(\ell+1)a_\ell q_{\ell+1}
\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right]\\
&=
\frac{a_\ell}{n}
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left[
(1-\rho)(n-\ell)q_\ell
-\sqrt{\rho(1-\rho)}(\ell+1)q_{\ell+1}
\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right],
\end{align*}
where we observe in the last step that the square bracket coefficient vanishes. Multiplying by $H_\ell$ and using
$\sqrt{\rho(1-\rho)}a_\ell=\rho a_{\ell+1}$ gives
\begin{align*}
\mathbb{E}_{x\in C}[(1-s(x))H_\ell(x)^2]
&=
\frac{(1-\rho)(n-\ell)}{n}
\sum_{j=0}^{\ell}a_\ell a_j\mathbb{E}_{x\in C}[q_\ell(x)q_j(x)]-
\frac{\rho(\ell+1)}{n}
\sum_{j=0}^{\ell}a_{\ell+1}a_j
\mathbb{E}_{x\in C}[q_{\ell+1}(x)q_j(x)].
\end{align*}
Now, we expand both products $q_{\ell}(x) q_j(x)$ and $q_{\ell+1}(x)q_j(x)$ using \cref{lem:layer-recursion}, which says that
$q_jq_{j'}=\sum_{t=0}^nN_\rho(j,j';t)q_t$.
We get
\begin{align*}
    \mathbb{E}_{x \in C} [ (1 - s(x)) H_\ell(x)^2 ] & = \frac{(1 - \rho)(n - \ell)}{n} \sum_{j=0}^\ell a_\ell a_j  \sum_{t=0}^n N_\rho(\ell,j;t) \mathbb{E}_{x \in C}[q_t(x)] \\
    &\ \ \ - \frac{ \rho(\ell + 1)}{n} \sum_{j=0}^\ell a_{\ell + 1} a_j \sum_{t=0}^n N_\rho(\ell + 1, j;t) \mathbb{E}_{x \in C}[q_t(x)].
\end{align*}

Since $N_\rho(j,j';0)=\binom nj\mathbf{1}\{j=j'\}$, the summands with $t=0$ and any $j$ together contribute $a_\ell^2\binom n\ell=\pi_\ell$ to the first sum, ignoring the prefactor of $(1 - \rho)(n-\ell)/n$, and vanishes in the second sum.
For the summands with $t>0$, we follow the same proof as the first inequality: for $r\in\{\ell,\ell+1\}$, we have that
\[
\sum_{j=0}^{\ell}a_ra_j|N_\rho(r,j;t)|
\le
\sum_{j,j'=0}^{n}a_ja_{j'}\tilde N_\rho(j,j';t)
=b_\rho^t.
\]
similar to \cref{eq:truncation-proxy}. Just as \cref{eq:key-trunction-2}, we now have by \cref{lem:key-fourier} and \cref{eq:truncation-gap} that
\begin{align*}
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left|
\mathbb{E}_{x\in C}[(1-s(x))H_\ell(x)^2]
-\frac{(1-\rho)(n-\ell)}{n}\pi_\ell
\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right|\le
2\sum_{t=1}^{\min(n,2\ell+1)}
b_\rho^t\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left|\mathbb{E}_{x\in C}[q_t(x)]\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right|\le e^{-\Omega(n)}.
\end{align*}
Finally, the second bound in \cref{lem:key-truncation} follows \cref{eq:truncation-0} and that $(1-s(x))H_\ell(x)^2\ge0$, since
\[
0\le
\mathbb{E}_{x\in C}[(1-s(x))H_\ell(x)^2]
\le
\frac{(1-\rho)(n-\ell)}{n}\pi_\ell+e^{-\Omega(n)}
\le e^{-\Omega(n)},
\]
and $\pi_\ell \le e^{-\Omega(n)}$. This concludes the proof of \cref{lem:key-truncation}.
<!-- end proof-20 -->
<!-- lemma-17: body_md -->
\label{lem:terminal-dense-count}
Let $p\ge n$ be prime, let $R+\rho>1$, and let $C$ be a  $[n, k]$ GRS code of rate $R$ over $\mathbb{F}_p$.  Fix $\rho \in (0,1)$ and input lists $S_i$ with $|S_i|=\rho p$ for every $i$. Then uniformly over $v\in\mathbb{F}_p^n$,
\begin{equation}
\mathsf{F}_{\operatorname{OPI}}(v)=
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft|\frac{|(v+C)\cap\mathbf S|}{|C|\rho^n}-1\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright|
\le e^{-\Omega_{R,\rho}(n)}
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-17 -->
<!-- proof-21: body_md -->
First take $v=0$. Every imperfect point has
$1-s(x)\ge1/n$. By \cref{lem:key-truncation},
\[
\mathbb{E}_{x\in C}[\mathbf{1}_{\mathbf S^c}(x)H_\ell(x)^2]
\le
n\mathbb{E}_{x\in C}[(1-s(x))H_\ell(x)^2]
\le e^{-\Omega(n)}.
\]
Now, observe that
$H_\ell(x)=\rho^{-n/2}\pi_{\le\ell}$ 
for every $x\in\mathbf{S}$, as 
$g_i(x_i)=\sqrt{(1-\rho)/\rho}$ for every $i$. Therefore,
\begin{align*}
\pi_{\le\ell}^2\frac{|C\cap\mathbf S|}{|C|\rho^n}
=\mathbb{E}_{x\in C}[\mathbf{1}_{\mathbf S}(x)H_\ell(x)^2]
=\mathbb{E}_{x\in C}[H_\ell(x)^2]
-\mathbb{E}_{x\in C}[\mathbf{1}_{\mathbf S^c}(x)H_\ell(x)^2]=1+e^{-\Omega(n)}.
\end{align*}
by \cref{lem:key-truncation} and \cref{eq:truncation-0}, and the desired conclusion for $v=0$ follows.
For general $v$, apply the same argument to the translated
lists $S_i-v_i$. Their densities are unchanged, and
\[
\mathsf{F}_{\operatorname{OPI}}(v;C,\mathbf S)
=\mathsf{F}_{\operatorname{OPI}}(0;C,\mathbf S-v).\qedhere
\]
<!-- end proof-21 -->
<!-- proof-22: body_md -->
It suffices by \cref{prop:subsample}, applied also to translates
$v+C$, to establish a uniform relative-count bound for common-size
sublists. We collect the three cases.

For (1) and (3), take $t=\lceil\rho p\rceil$. Its actual density
$t/p=\rho+O(1/p)$ preserves the fixed density and rate gaps.
Part~(1) follows from
\cref{lem:positive-opi-above,lem:translated-opi-proxy}, and part~(3)
follows from \cref{lem:terminal-dense-count}.

For (2), choose the theorem's constant $\delta$ smaller than the
constant $\delta_0$ of \cref{lem:near-balanced-opi-below}, and take
\[
    t=\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left\lceil p\min\{\rho,1/2\}\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right\rceil.
\]
For sufficiently large $p$,
    $t/p\in[1/2-\delta_0,1/2+\delta_0]$,
so that lemma applies.

In every case, $t\le\min_i|S_i|$, and \cref{prop:subsample} transfers
the exponentially small relative error to the original normalization
$|C|\prod_i\rho_i$. Taking $v=0$ proves all three assertions.
<!-- end proof-22 -->
<!-- theorem-12: body_md -->
\label{thm:sjo-main}
Let $C \subset \mathbb{F}_p^n$ be a linear code.  Suppose some deterministic classical algorithm that takes as input a received word $r \in \mathbb{F}_p^n$ and returns the set $\mathcal{L}(r) = \{c \in C^\perp : |r-c| \leq \ell\}$, with running time $T = T(n, \log p)$. 

If $|\mathcal{L}(r)| \leq L$ for every $r \in \mathbb{F}_p^n$, then there is a quantum algorithm that prepares $\ket{\varphi_\ell}$ exactly under coherent membership oracle access to the input lists $S_i$ with known common density $\rho\in (0, 1)$, and with running time $\operatorname{poly}(T,n,\log p)$ and flagged success probability $\frac{Z_\ell}{LZ_0}$.
<!-- end theorem-12 -->
<!-- lemma-18: body_md -->
\label{lem:z-partial-check}
For any $0< \alpha \le 1-\rho$ with $\alpha <R$, let $\ell=\lfloor \alpha n\rfloor$ and define $\ket{\varphi_\ell}, F_\ell$ as in \cref{eq:dqi-state}. Then, $Z_0=\sigma+1$ and $Z_\ell = \sigma+1+e^{-\Omega(n)}$. Moreover, the expected satisfaction ratio of the outcome $x$ obtained by measuring the DQI state $\ket{\varphi_\ell}$ is $\operatorname{SCL}_\rho(\alpha)+o(1)$.
<!-- end lemma-18 -->
<!-- proof-23: body_md -->
By orthogonality in \cref{eq:sw-local-and-orthogonality}, we see that $Z_0 = \sigma + 1$.
We verify \cref{eq:key-decay} to apply \cref{thm:sw-main} to bound $Z_\ell$ and the expected satisfaction ratio. Let
$\pi_j(\alpha):=\mathbb{P}(B=j)=\binom nj\alpha^j(1-\alpha)^{n-j}$ for $B\sim \operatorname{Bin}(n,\alpha)$. Note that $h(t):=2\sqrt{t(1-t)}+t|\beta_\rho|$ is nondecreasing for $t\in [0, 1-\rho]$, so it attains maximum at $h(1-\rho)=b_\rho$. 
Now, substituting $z=\sqrt{\alpha/(1-\alpha)}$ into
\cref{eq:N-bound} gives
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:b-rho-expansion}
\frac{\tilde N_\rho(j,j';t)}
{\sqrt{\binom nj\binom n{j'}}}
\le
\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(2\sqrt{\alpha(1-\alpha)}+\alpha|\beta_\rho|\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)^t}
{\sqrt{\pi_j(\alpha)\pi_{j'}(\alpha)}}
\le\frac{b_\rho^t}{\sqrt{\pi_j(\alpha)\pi_{j'}(\alpha)}}=\Theta( b_\rho^t \sqrt{n}),
\end{equation}
where we justify the last step as follows: for $j, j'\in [\ell-\sigma-1, \ell+1]$, recalling that $\sigma = \lfloor \log\log n \rfloor$, we have that $|j-\alpha n|=O(\log\log n)$, so Stirling's formula gives $\pi_j(\alpha)=\Theta (n^{-1/2})$ and $\pi_{j'}(\alpha)=\Theta (n^{-1/2})$ uniformly for such pairs $j, j'$. Thus, by \cref{lem:key-fourier}, for every pair in
\cref{eq:key-decay} and every $k<t\le\min\{n,j+j'\}$,
\[
\frac{\tilde N_\rho(j,j';t)}
{\sqrt{\binom nj\binom n{j'}}}
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft|\mathbb{E}_{x\in C}q_t(x)\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright|
\le O(\sqrt n)
\sum_{\substack{y\in C^\perp\\|y|=t}}
b_\rho^{|y|}\prod_{i\in\operatorname{supp}(y)}|\widehat g_i(y_i)|
\le e^{-\Omega(n)},
\]
where the last inequality is by \cref{lem:N-proxy-bound}. Its support assumption holds uniformly here since $t\le\min(n,2\ell+2)$ and $2k-(2\ell+2)=2(R-\alpha)n-O(1)=\Omega(n)$. Now, \cref{thm:sw-main} gives
$Z_\ell=\sigma+1+O(e^{-\Omega(n)})$ and expected satisfaction ratio
$\mathsf{SCL}_\rho(\ell/n)+o(1)=\mathsf{SCL}_\rho(\alpha)+o(1)$.
<!-- end proof-23 -->
<!-- proof-24: body_md -->
First suppose that all lists have common exact density $\rho$. Since $C^\perp$ has rate $1-R$ and $\alpha<R$ with a fixed
gap, \cref{thm:rs-ld,thm:rs-lr} give a deterministic list decoder
with polynomial running time and a uniform list bound $L=n^{O(1)}$, as required by \cref{thm:sjo-main}.
By \cref{lem:z-partial-check,eq:def-partial-alpha}, measuring
$\ket{\varphi_\ell}$ gives expected satisfaction at least
$\mathsf{SCL}_\rho(R)-\varepsilon/2$ for sufficiently large $n$.
Since satisfaction lies in $[0,1]$, the probability of obtaining
satisfaction at least $\mathsf{SCL}_\rho(R)-\varepsilon$ is at least
$\varepsilon/2$ by applying Markov's inequality to $1-s(x)$.

 \cref{lem:z-partial-check} implies that $Z_\ell/Z_0\ge1/2$, so the algorithm from \cref{thm:sjo-main} prepares the state $\ket{\varphi_\ell}$ successfully with
probability at least $1/(2L)$. 
By the above reasoning, 
this returns a solution
with satisfaction at least $\mathsf{SCL}_\rho(R) - \varepsilon$ with probability at least
$\varepsilon/(4L)$. The final algorithm then runs this algorithm $T = O(L\log n)$ times independently, 
 evaluates the successful outputs' scores exactly, and
retains the best. The fact that $L = n^{O(1)}$ gives
the claimed running time, and shows that the success probability is at least
\[ 1 - \mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left( 1 - \frac{ \varepsilon }{4L } \aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)^T \geq 1 - \frac{1}{n^{\Omega(\varepsilon)}} = 1 - o(1).\]

Finally, if lists sizes satisfy $|S_i|\ge \rho p$, the common-size algorithm above succeeds on every tuple of $\rho p$-element lists. For truth-table input, trim each list to size $\rho p$; for oracle input, apply \cref{prop:oracle-thinning} to this common-size algorithm. Both reductions preserve satisfaction on the original lists and give the claimed high-probability guarantee in polynomial time.
<!-- end proof-24 -->
<!-- proof-25: body_md -->
By thinning to a common list size (see \cref{subsec:subsampling}), it suffices to consider a common
density  $\rho$.
Choose a fixed $\eta\in(0,R-\rho)$ sufficiently small relative to $\varepsilon$, and set
\[
    t:=\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left\lfloor
          \frac{2(R-\eta-\rho)}{1-2\rho} n\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right\rfloor\]
Fix any set $T\in\binom{[n]}{t}$, let $N:=n-t$, and let $\ell:=\lfloor\rho N\rfloor$. Then, we see that
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:AT-gap}
N=\Theta(n)\quad\text{and}\quad 
t+2\ell=2(R-\eta)n\le 2k-\Omega(n).
\end{equation}

Now, let $C|_{T^c} \subset \mathbb{F}_p^{T^c}$ be the puncturing of $C$ to the coordinates indexed by $T^c$.
Consider the OPI instance given by $C|_{T^c}$ with the corresponding input lists $S_i$ for $i\in T^c$. We will consider the DQI state $\ket{\varphi_\ell}$ on this instance with parameters $\ell:=\lfloor\rho N\rfloor, \sigma:=\lfloor \log \log N\rfloor$.
That is,  let
\[ \ket{\varphi_\ell} \propto \sum_{x \in C|_{T^c}} F_\ell(x) \ket{x},\]
where
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:puncture-param}
F_\ell(x):=\sum_{j=\ell-\sigma}^\ell\frac{q_j(x)}{\sqrt{\binom Nj}}\quad\text{where}\quad q_j(x):=\sum_{B\in\binom{T^c}j}\prod_{i\in B}g_i(x_i)\quad\text{and}\quad  g_i(x_i):=
    \frac{\mathbf{1}_{S_i}(x_i)-\rho}
         {\sqrt{\rho(1-\rho)}}.
\end{equation}
Here, we abuse notation and overload $F_\ell, q_j, g_i$ to refer to the versions defined on the punctured instance for the rest of this proof. However, we distinguish between the satisfaction ratios \[
\tilde{s}(x):=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i\not\in T}\mathbf{1}_{S_i}(x_i)\quad\text{and}\quad s(x)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n\mathbf{1}_{S_i}(x_i).
\]
We consider the distribution $\mu$ on $C$ where $\mu(x) \propto A_T(x)F_\ell(x)^2$, with $ A_T(x):=\rho^{-t}\prod_{i\in T}\mathbf{1}\{x_i\in S_i\}$.
We think of $\mu$ as the distribution on $x \in C$ (or equivalently, on polynomials of degree less than $k$) given by the DQI state $\ket{\varphi_\ell}$ for the punctured instance to coordinates $T^c$, conditioned on satisfying every constraint in $T$. 
For $h:\mathbb{F}_p^n\to \mathbb{R}$, we adopt shorthand notation $\mathbb{E} h:=\mathbb{E}_{x\in \mathbb{F}_p^n}[h(x)]$ and $\mathbb{E}_C h :=\mathbb{E}_{x\in C}[h(x)]$.
Then, the expected satisfaction $s(x)$ for $x\sim\mu$ is given by
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:def-tilt}
\mathbb{E}_{x\sim \mu} [s(x)]=
\frac{\mathbb{E}_{C}[sA_TF_\ell^2]}{\mathbb{E}_{C}[A_TF_\ell^2]}=\frac{t}{n}+\frac{n-t}{n}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{C}[\tilde{s}A_TF_\ell^2]}{\mathbb{E}_{C}[A_TF_\ell^2]}
\end{equation}

To compute the numerator and denominator, we compute them first over $x\in\mathbb{F}_p^n$ rather than $x\in C$, and argue that passing from the uniform distribution on $\mathbb{F}_p^n$ to that on $C$ changes the expectations by an exponentially small amount. By \cref{eq:sw-local-and-orthogonality}, and by the fact that $q_j, q_j$ depend only on the coordinates $x_i$ for $i\in T^c$ while $A_T$ depends only on coordinates $x_i$ for $i\in T$, we see that
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ambient-AT}
\mathbb{E}[A_Tq_jq_{j'}]=\mathbb{E}[q_jq_{j'}]=\binom Nj \mathbf{1}\{j=j'\}.
\end{equation}
Therefore, conditioning on $A_T$ preserves the expectations over $\mathbb{F}_p^n$, i.e.
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ambient-scl}
\begin{aligned}
\mathbb{E}[A_TF_\ell^2]&=\mathbb{E}[F_\ell^2]=\sigma+1,\\
\mathbb{E}[\tilde{s}A_TF_\ell^2]&=\mathbb{E}[\tilde{s}F_\ell^2] =[\operatorname{SCL}_\rho(\rho)+o(1)](\sigma+1).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where the semicircle law is evaluated at $\rho$ since $\ell\sim\rho N$. The equalities follow
\cref{eq:sw-local-and-orthogonality} and the three-term recurrence in
\cref{lem:layer-recursion}.
Next, recall \cref{lem:N-proxy-bound}. As $\rho\le 1/2$ by assumption, $b_\rho =\lambda=\sqrt{(1-\rho)/\rho}$. Now, apply the proof of \cref{lem:z-partial-check} verbatim to the punctured instance and with $N$ in place of $n$ and $\alpha=\rho$ to obtain the following analog of \cref{eq:b-rho-expansion}: for $\ell-\sigma-1\le j, j'\le \ell+1$,
\[
    \frac{q_j(x)q_{j'}(x)}{\sqrt{\binom Nj\binom N{j'}}}
    =
    \sum_{B\subset T^c}c_B\prod_{i\in B}g_i(x_i),
\]
where $c_\emptyset=\mathbf{1}\{j=j'\}$, $c_B=0$ if $|B|>j+j'$, and otherwise $|c_B|=O(b_\rho^{|B|}\sqrt{N}) = O(\lambda^{|B|}\sqrt{N})$.
Observe that $A_T(x)
    =
    \prod_{i\in T}(1+\lambda g_i(x_i))$ by definition of $g_i$. Applying this and taking expectations over $x\in C$, 
\[
 \frac{\mathbb{E}_{C}[A_Tq_jq_{j'}]}
           {\sqrt{\binom Nj\binom N{j'}}} = c_\emptyset+\sum_{\emptyset\ne D\subset [n]}\lambda^{|D\cap T|}c_{D\cap T^c}\mathbb{E}_{x\in C}\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left[\prod_{i\in D}g_i(x_i)\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right].
\]
Now, we obtain the key comparison inequality that says switching distributions from $x\in\mathbb{F}_p^n$ to $x\in C$ incurs exponentially small error for the relevant expectations in \cref{eq:def-tilt}:
\begin{align*}
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left|\frac{\mathbb{E}_{C}[A_Tq_jq_{j'}]-\mathbb{E}[A_Tq_jq_{j'}]}
           {\sqrt{\binom Nj\binom N{j'}}} \aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right| & =\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left|\frac{\mathbb{E}_{C}[A_Tq_jq_{j'}]}
           {\sqrt{\binom Nj\binom N{j'}}} -\mathbf{1}\{j=j'\}\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right| 
           \\ & \le
  O(\sqrt{N})
    \sum_{\substack{0\ne y\in C^\perp\\|y|\le t+j+j'}}
       \prod_{i\in\operatorname{supp}(y)}
          \lambda|\widehat g_i(y_i)|
          \\ & \le e^{-\Omega(n)}.
\end{align*}
We justify the steps as follows: the first step holds by \cref{eq:ambient-AT}, the second step follows from \cref{lem:key-fourier} and our computation of $c_B$ above, and the last step is given by \cref{lem:N-proxy-bound} where we check its hypothesis holds by \cref{eq:AT-gap} and that $j, j'\le \ell+1\sim\rho N$, so $2k-(t+j+j')\ge2\eta n-O(1)$. Now, by expanding $F_\ell^2$ and $\tilde{s}F_\ell^2$ in terms of $q_j$ and $q_{j'}$ via \cref{lem:layer-recursion}, we see that 
\[
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left|\mathbb{E}_C[\tilde{s}A_TF_\ell^2] - \mathbb{E}[\tilde{s}A_TF_\ell^2]\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right|\le e^{-\Omega(n)} \quad\text{and}\quad
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left|\mathbb{E}_C[A_TF_\ell^2]-\mathbb{E}[A_TF_\ell^2]\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right|\le e^{-\Omega(n)}.
\]
Combining with \cref{eq:ambient-scl} and \cref{eq:def-tilt}, we see that
\begin{align*}
\mathbb{E}_{x\sim \mu} [s(x)]& = \frac{t}{n}+\frac{n-t}{n}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{C}[\tilde{s}A_TF_\ell^2]}{\mathbb{E}_{C}[A_TF_\ell^2]}
\\ & =  \frac tn+\frac {n-t}n \operatorname{SCL}_\rho(\rho)+o(1)\\
    &=  1-\frac {n-t}n(1-2\rho)^2+o(1)\\
    &=
    1-(1-2\rho)(1-2R)-2(1-2\rho)\eta+o(1).
\end{align*}
where the computation follows our choice of parameters of $\ell$ and $t$. Hence, some $x\in C$ has score
\[
    s(x)\ge
    1-(1-2\rho)(1-2R)-\varepsilon,
\]
upon choosing $\eta$ sufficiently small and then $n$ sufficiently large. This proves the theorem.
<!-- end proof-25 -->
<!-- remark-8: body_md -->
\label{rem:exist-no-algo}
The crucial idea in the proof is the puncturing to $T^c$ and conditioning via $A_T$. In comparison to the proof of \cref{thm:main-opi}(3) (near-optimal OPI discrepancy in the case when $R + \rho > 1$), which also uses \cref{lem:N-proxy-bound}, here $A_TF_\ell^2$ has support degree at most $t+2\ell$---rather than $2(t+\ell)$---by paying for the coordinates in $T$ once. We are then able to apply \cref{lem:N-proxy-bound} with $t+2\ell<2k-\Omega(n)$ even when $t+\ell>k$. This is the parameter regime in \cref{thm:main-existential}, and why the proof of \cref{thm:main-opi}(3) for $R+\rho>1$ does not apply.

This parameter regime also explains why the satisfaction ratio in \cref{thm:main-existential} cannot immediately be attained quantum algorithmically.
One might be tempted to use the algorithm in \cref{sec:algo-recap} to prepare the state whose corresponding distribution is $\mu$. This state has amplitudes given by $\sqrt{A_T}F_\ell$, and directly using that implementation would require list decoding of $C^\perp$ through radius $t+\ell$. The dual has rate $1-R$, so the list-decoding capacity theorem implies that it cannot be reliably list-decoded (with polynomial list sizes) beyond radius $R$.  On the other hand, our parameters give
\[
    \frac{t+\ell}{n}
    =
    R-\eta+\frac{t}{2n}>R
\]
for sufficiently small fixed $\eta$.
Therefore, the associated list sizes in the list-decoding steps are then exponentially large, and our implementation in \cref{sec:algo-recap} fails to prepare this state efficiently.
<!-- end remark-8 -->
<!-- lemma-19: body_md -->
\label{eq:perfect-prob}
Fix $\ell$ from \cref{eq:def-perfect-alpha} and $\ket{\varphi_\ell}$ from \cref{eq:dqi-state}. Then, the probability of obtaining a perfect solution upon measuring $\ket{\varphi_\ell}$ is
\begin{equation}
(1+o(1))\frac{\sigma+1}{\sqrt{2\pi n\rho(1-\rho)}}\ge n^{-O(1)}
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-19 -->
<!-- proof-26: body_md -->
For every ``perfect'' codeword $x\in C\cap\mathbf S$, all coordinates $i \in [n]$
satisfy $g_i(x_i)=\sqrt{(1-\rho)/\rho}$. Thus, with the
binomial probabilities $\pi_j$ defined in \cref{eq:def-pi-j},
\[
F_\ell(x)
=\sum_{j=\ell-\sigma}^{\ell}
\sqrt{\binom nj}\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(\frac{1-\rho}{\rho}\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)^{j/2}
=\rho^{-n/2}\sum_{j=\ell-\sigma}^{\ell}\sqrt{\pi_j}.
\]
Since the squared norm of $\sum_{x\in C}F_\ell(x)\ket{x}$
is $|C|Z_\ell$, the probability of a perfect measurement is
\[
\frac{|C\cap\mathbf S|}{|C|\rho^n}
\frac1{Z_\ell}
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(\sum_{j=\ell-\sigma}^{\ell}\sqrt{\pi_j}\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)^2.
\]
The first factor is $1+O(e^{-\Omega(n)})$ by
\cref{lem:terminal-dense-count}. Because $1-\rho<R$,
\cref{lem:z-partial-check} applies at $\alpha=1-\rho$ and gives
$Z_\ell=\sigma+1+O(e^{-\Omega(n)})$.
Each $j$ in the summand satisfies $|j-(1-\rho)n|=O(\log\log n)$, so Stirling's formula gives that, uniformly over all $j$
\[
\pi_j=\frac{1+o(1)}{\sqrt{2\pi n\rho(1-\rho)}}.
\]
The squared sum is therefore
$\sim(\sigma+1)^2/\sqrt{2\pi n\rho(1-\rho)}$, which proves the desired bound.
<!-- end proof-26 -->
<!-- proof-27: body_md -->
First suppose that all lists have common exact density $\rho$.
Since $R+\rho>1$, the choice
$\ell=\lfloor(1-\rho)n\rfloor$ satisfies $\ell<k-\Omega(n)$.
Thus \cref{thm:rs-ld} supplies the decoder required by
\cref{thm:sjo-main}, with $L=\operatorname{poly}(n)$.

By \cref{lem:z-partial-check}, $Z_\ell/Z_0\ge1/2$, so each
preparation succeeds with probability at least $1/(2L)$.
Conditioned on success, \cref{eq:perfect-prob} implies that measurement yields a perfect solution with probability
$n^{-O(1)}$.
Hence each attempt returns a perfect solution with
inverse-polynomial probability $n^{-O(1)}$.  Polynomially many independent
attempts, followed by exact verification of every constraint,
give a perfect solution with high probability and the claimed
running time.

Finally, if lists have different sizes but $|S_i|\ge \rho p$ for all $i$, the common-size algorithm described above above succeeds on every tuple of $\rho p$-element lists. For truth-table input, trim each list to size $\rho p$; for oracle input, apply \cref{prop:oracle-thinning} to this common-size algorithm. Both reductions preserve satisfaction on the original lists and give the claimed high-probability guarantee in polynomial time.
<!-- end proof-27 -->
<!-- remark-9: body_md -->
As an alternative proof of \cref{thm:main-algo}(2), 
could instead sample the state with amplitudes $H_\ell(x)$ from the proof of \cref{thm:main-opi}(3), defined in \cref{eq:def-H-ell}, where  $(1-\rho)n+\Omega(n)\le\ell\le k-\Omega(n)$. By \cref{lem:key-truncation}, measuring it attains a perfect solution with high probability, since 
\[ \frac{n\mathbb{E}_C[(1-s)H_\ell^2]}{\mathbb{E}_C[H_\ell^2]}=e^{-\Omega(n)}.\]
The proof of \cref{thm:sjo-main} (adapted to use the coefficients defining $H_\ell$) implies that we can prepare this state efficiently.  The reason that we prove the theorem as we do (with coefficients from the original DQI state) is for simplicity, so that \cref{thm:sjo-main} applies in its stated form.
<!-- end remark-9 -->
<!-- definition-7: body_md -->
\label{def:source-ensemble}
Let $m+1$ be a power of two, let $\mathbb{F}_q$ be the finite field with order $q=(m+1)^2$, and choose an alphabet $ \Sigma=\mathbb{F}_q^{m+2}$. The code $C_0\subset \Sigma^m$ is given by evaluating degree polynomials $Q\in\mathbb{F}_q[X]$ with $\deg Q\le9(q-1)/10$ at consecutive
powers of a primitive element of $\mathbb{F}_q$, and folding consecutive $m+2$
evaluations.
That is, if $\gamma$ is the chosen primitive element,
\[ C_0 = \mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left\{ \mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left( \begin{bmatrix} Q(1) \\ Q(\gamma) \\ \vdots \\ Q(\gamma^{m+1}) 
\end{bmatrix}, 
\begin{bmatrix} Q(\gamma^{m+2}) \\ Q(\gamma^{m+3}) \\ \vdots \\ Q(\gamma^{2m + 3})
\end{bmatrix}, \cdots,
\begin{bmatrix} Q(\gamma^{(m-1)(m+2)}) \\ Q(\gamma^{(m-1)(m+2) + 1}) \\ \vdots \\ Q(\gamma^{m(m+2) - 1}) 
\end{bmatrix}
\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right) \,:\, \deg(Q) \leq 9(q-1)/10 \aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right\} \subset \Sigma^m.
\]
The ensemble is given by $C_0$ as well as by 
a tuple $O=(O_1,\ldots,O_m)$ of independent uniform
functions $O_j:\Sigma\to\{0,1\}$, interpreted as membership oracles for the input lists.
<!-- end definition-7 -->
<!-- theorem-13: body_md -->
\label{thm:advantage-yz}
For the ensemble in \cref{def:source-ensemble} and all sufficiently large $m$:
\begin{enumerate}
\item There is a uniform polynomial-time quantum algorithm $\mathcal{Q}^O$ that finds a perfect source
message with probability $1-e^{-\Omega(m)}$ over $O$ and the algorithm.
\item Every randomized classical algorithm $\mathcal A^O$ making at most $2^{m^{2/3}}-m$ queries to $O$ satisfies
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:def-eta}
\mathbb{P}_{O,\mathcal A}\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(
r(\mathcal A^O)\in [0, \eta)\cup (1-\eta, 1]
\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)\le e^{-\Omega(m)}\quad\text{where}\quad \eta:=\frac{1}{80}.
\end{equation}
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end theorem-13 -->
<!-- proof-28: body_md -->
The quantum algorithm is~\cite[Lemma~6.3]{yz}. For the classical claim, use~\cite[Lemma~4.2 and Section~4.3.2]{yz}
with list-recovery exponents $2/3$ and $3/4$ and agreement fraction $19/20$. We apply the proof
of \cite[Lemma~6.9]{yz} with two-sided Chernoff bound to handle both tails in \cref{eq:def-eta}.
In both statements, the failure probability bound of \cite{yz} is given as $\operatorname{negl}(m)$, but a closer inspection of the proofs review it is actually $e^{-\Omega(m)}$.
<!-- end proof-28 -->
<!-- definition-8: body_md -->
\label{def:sparse-target}
Fix any $k\in [n]$ and any Reed-Solomon code $C\subset \mathbb{F}_p^n$ of dimension $k$. Sample an instance from the ensemble in \cref{def:source-ensemble}, given by oracles $O=(O_1, \dots, O_m)$ with $m=n$. 
Construct the OPI instance given by $C$ and membership oracles $F_1, \dots, F_n$ of the input lists as follows. Define functions $\varphi_O, \varphi_{\operatorname{all}}:\mathbb{F}_p\to \{0, 1\}$ via
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:advantage-warmup-predicates}
\varphi_O(u)=O_{j(u)}(E_{j(u)}(a(u)))\quad\text{and}\quad 
\varphi_{\operatorname{all}}(u)=\prod_{j=1}^m O_j(E_j(a(u))).
\end{equation}
Then, let $F_i=\varphi_{\operatorname{all}}$ for every $i\in [n-1]$ and $F_n=\varphi_O$.
<!-- end definition-8 -->
<!-- theorem-14: body_md -->
\label{thm:advantage-warmup}
For sufficiently large $n$,
the ensemble in \cref{def:sparse-target} admits a uniform polynomial time quantum algorithm
that returns a perfect polynomial with probability
$1-e^{-\Omega(n)}$. 

Moreover, every classical polynomial-query algorithm returns
a polynomial satisfying more than one constraint with probability at most
$e^{-\Omega(n)}$.
<!-- end theorem-14 -->
<!-- proof-29: body_md -->
For the quantum algorithm, we solve the underlying instance from \cref{def:source-ensemble} via the quantum algorithm in \cref{thm:advantage-yz}. To simulate oracle query $O_j(z_j)$ for $j\in [m]$ and some $z_j\in \Sigma$, we efficiently find via \cref{eq:advantage-encoder} some $a\in \mathbb{F}_2^D$ with $E_j(a)=z_j$, compute
$u=u(j, a)\in \mathbb{F}_p$ via \cref{eq:advantage-embedding}, and return the query outcome
\[ F_n(u) = O_{j(u)}(E_{j(u)}(a(u))) = O_j(z_j).\]
With probability $1-e^{-\Omega(n)}$, we find some perfects solution $z\in C_0$ to the corresponding instance in \cref{def:source-ensemble}, i.e. $O_j(z_j)=1$ for every $j$. Then, 
we find via \cref{eq:advantage-encoder} some $a\in \mathbb{F}_2^D$ with $E(a)=z$, and output the constant polynomial $Q(X)=u$ where $u=u(1, a)\in \mathbb{F}_p$  via \cref{eq:advantage-embedding}. We see that this polynomial satisfies every constraint $F_i$ by definition \cref{eq:advantage-warmup-predicates}.

Conversely, we efficiently simulate each classical target query $F_i(u)$ as follows: compute $j(u), a(u)$ via \cref{eq:advantage-parser}, and query $O_j(E_j(a(u)))$ for every $j\in [m]$ via \cref{eq:advantage-encoder}. 
Then, $F_n(u)$ is the $j=j(u)$ outcome and $F_i(u)$ is the product of the $m$ outcomes for every $i\in [n-1]$.
Hence, any efficient classical algorithm on the target ensemble of \cref{def:sparse-target} can be turned into an efficient classical algorithm on the source ensemble of \cref{def:source-ensemble}. If on some instances of the target ensemble the output polynomial $Q$ satisfies constraint $F_i(Q(\alpha_i))=1$ for some $i\in [n-1]$, find such an $i$ by querying these $n-1$ constraints, and compute $a=a(Q(\alpha_i))\in\mathbb{F}_2^D$ via \cref{eq:advantage-parser}. Then, $r(a)=1$ by \cref{eq:advantage-warmup-predicates}, i.e. $E(a)$ is a perfect solution for the corresponding source instance. Now, the classical hardness in \cref{thm:advantage-warmup} follows that in \cref{thm:advantage-yz}.
<!-- end proof-29 -->
<!-- definition-9: body_md -->
\label{def:balanced-target}
Fix the parameters in
\cref{eq:def-p-new,eq:def-param}. We define an ensemble of OPI instances on code $C\subset \mathbb{F}_p^n$ of dimension $k$ as follows. Index the $n$ coordinates by
\begin{equation}
I:=\{O, B\}\cup \mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left( \binom{[m]}t \times [H]\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right).
\end{equation}
We describe \emph{setup randomness} $S$: independently of the source oracle $O$, and independent of each other, sample the following:
\begin{itemize}
    \item Sample evaluation points $\boldsymbol{\alpha}:=\{\alpha_i:i\in I\}$ without replacement from $\mathbb{F}_p$, and let $C:=\operatorname{RS}_p(\boldsymbol{\alpha};k)$.
    \item Sample random bits
$B(u)\sim\operatorname{Ber}(\delta)$ for each $u\in \mathcal{U}:=\{0, \dots, (p-3)/2\}$. If $B(u) = 1$, we refer to $u$ as \emph{active}
\item Sample random bits
$C_{J,h}(u)\sim \operatorname{Ber}(1/2)$ for each $u\in \mathcal{U}$ and $(J, h)\in \binom{[m]}{t}\times [H]$.
\end{itemize}

For each setup $S=(\boldsymbol{\alpha}, B, C)$, define $\varphi_i :\mathcal{U}\to\{0, 1\}$ for $i\in I$ as follows: let \begin{equation}
\label{eq:advantage-OB-predicates}
\varphi_O(u):=O_{j(u)}(E_{j(u)}(a(u))) \quad \text{and}\quad \varphi_B(u):=B(u),
\end{equation}
and for each $(J, h)\in\binom{[m]}{t}\times [H]$ let
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:advantage-label-predicates}
\varphi_{J,h}(u):=
\begin{cases}
\mathbf{1}\{ \#\{j\in J: O_{j}(E_{j}(a(u)))=0\} \text{ is even}\},
&\text{if }B(u)=1,\\
C_{J,h}(u), &\text{if }B(u)=0.
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
For each $i\in I$, we define the input lists given by membership oracles $F_i:\mathbb{F}_p\to \{0, 1\}$ by
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:advantage-balanced-predicates}
F_i(2u+b):=\varphi_i(u)\oplus b,
\end{equation}
for $u\in \mathcal{U}$ and $b\in\{0, 1\}$, and let
$F_i(p-1)=0$.
<!-- end definition-9 -->
<!-- proposition-3: body_md -->
\label{prop:consistency}
With probability $1-e^{-\Omega(m)}$ over the setup $S=(\boldsymbol{\alpha}, B, C)$, the following
holds simultaneously for every source oracle $O$ and every
polynomial $Q\in\mathbb{F}_p[X]$ with $\deg Q<k$: if $s(Q)>1/2+\varepsilon$, then $Q(X)=2u$ is constant for some $u\in\mathcal{U}$ such that $B(u)=1$ and
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:advantage-consistency-conclusion}
r(a(u))\in [0, \eta)\cup (1-\eta, 1].
\end{equation}
Moreover, if $Q(X)=2u$ with $B(u)=1$ and $r(a(u))=1$, then $s(Q)=1$.
<!-- end proposition-3 -->
<!-- proof-30: body_md -->
If $Q(X)=2u$ with $B(u)=1$ and $r(a(u))=1$, then $\varphi_O(u)=1$ as $O_j(E_j(a(u)))=1$ for every $j$, and so $\varphi_{J, h}(u)=1$ for every $(J, h)$. Moreover, $\varphi_B(u)=B(u)=1$. Hence, $F_i(2u)=\varphi_i(u)=1$ for every $i\in I$, so $s(Q)=1$. For the rest of the proof, we show that with high probability over setup $S=(\boldsymbol{\alpha}, B, C)$, every $Q$ with $s(Q)>1/2+\varepsilon$ has the form asserted in \cref{eq:advantage-consistency-conclusion}, simultaneously for every realization $O$ in the source ensemble. We will then union bound over $Q$, agnostic over $O$.

Suppose that $Q$ is nonconstant, for each ``main coordinate'' $i\in \binom{[m]}{t}\times[H]$, write $Q(\alpha_i)$ uniquely as $Q(\alpha_i)=2u_i+b_i$ for some $u_i\in\mathcal{U}$ and $b_i\in\{0, 1\}$. First, we show that it is unlikely for there to be at most $(1-\delta)N$ many distinct $u_i$'s. That is, it is unlikely that $|\{ u_i: i \in [N] \}| \leq (1 - \delta)N$.
Fix any set $U\subset \mathcal{U}$ of size $(1-\delta)N$ and any nonconstant polynomial $Q$. Then, for every $i\in \binom{[m]}{t}\times[H]$, $Q(\alpha_i)$ must be one of at most $2|U|+1\le 3N$ fixed values in $\mathbb{F}_p$ since each $u\in U$ corresponds $2u$ and $2u+1$, with possibly additionally $p-1$. Now, for random evaluations $\boldsymbol{\alpha}$, by counting roots, 
\[
\mathbb{P}_S\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(u_i\in U\,\forall\, i\in \binom{[m]}{t}\times[H]\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)\le \frac{(2Nk)^N}{p(p-1)\dots (p-N+1)}.
\]
Now, we union bound over $Q, U$ and apply our parameter choices \cref{eq:def-p-new,eq:def-param} to obtain
\begin{align*}
\mathbb{P}_S\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(\exists \text{ nonconstant $Q$ with }\le (1-\delta)N\text{ distinct }u_i\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right) & \le \frac{p^{k+(1-\delta)N}(2Nk)^N}{p(p-1)\dots (p-N+1)}
\\ & \le
\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left((k-\delta N)\log p+N\log(2Nk)+O(N^2/p)\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)
\\ & =e^{-\Omega(\delta N\log p)}
\\ & \le e^{-\Omega (m)}.
\end{align*}

Second, assume that some nonconstant $Q$ has more than $(1-\delta)N$ distinct $u_i$'s; collect these values of $u_i$ into $U\subset \mathcal{U}$.  We show that it is unlikely for there to be more than $3\delta N$ many main coordinates $i$ where $B(u_i)=1$. Indeed, if the $N$ choices of $u_i$ were all distinct, we would have $\operatorname{Bin}(N, \delta)$ such $i$. Since there are at most $N-|U|\le \delta N$ duplicate labels $u_i$ by the first step, the number of main coordinates $i$ where $B(u_i)=1$ is at most $\delta N+\operatorname{Bin}(N, \delta)$. Temporarily letting $Z_2\sim \operatorname{Bin}(N, \delta)$, we see that 
\begin{align*}
\mathbb{P}_S\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(\exists \text{ nonconstant $Q$ with $\ge 3\delta N$ many $i\in \binom{[m]}{t}\times[H]$ where $B(u_i)=1$}\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right) 
& \le e^{-\Omega(m)}+p^k\mathbb{P}(Z_2\ge 2\delta N)
\\ & \le e^{-\Omega(m)} +p^ke^{-2\delta^2 N}
\\ & \le e^{-\Omega(m)} +e^{-\Omega(\delta^2 N)}
\\ & \le e^{-\Omega(m)} 
\end{align*}

                                                                                                                                                    
                                                                
Third, suppose that some nonconstant $Q$ satisfies the first two steps, so $Q$ has at least $(1 - \delta)N$ distinct values of $u_i$, and that there are at most $3 \delta N$ main active coordinates $i$.  
Let
\[ I':=\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left\{i\in \binom{[m]}{t}\times[H]:B(u_i)=1\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right\}\]
be this set of main active coordinates.

For every main coordinate $i\notin I'$, $B(u_i)=0$ so i.i.d. random bits $C_i(u_i)$ are used. Then, the number of such constraints satisfied by each $Q$ is bounded by $\operatorname{Bin}(N-|I'|, 1/2)$, conditional on $\boldsymbol{\alpha}$ and $B$. For the constraints $i\in I'$, we assume they automatically hold, which allows us to bound the satisfaction ratio simultaneously for every $O$ as follows:
for such $Q$, $n\cdot s(Q)\le |I'|+2+Z_3$ where $Z_3\sim \operatorname{Bin}(N, 1/2)$ temporarily, so
$s(Q)\ge\frac12+\varepsilon$ implies 
\[Z_3
    \ge
    \mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(\frac12+\varepsilon\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)(N+2)-3\delta N-2
    \ge
    \frac N2+\delta N,\]
since $\delta=\varepsilon/100$. Therefore, we see that
\begin{align*}
\mathbb{P}_S\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(\exists\, O, \exists \text{ nonconstant $Q$ with }s(Q)\ge \frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right) 
& \le e^{-\Omega(m)}+p^k\mathbb{P}(Z_3\ge N/2+\delta N)
\\ & \le e^{-\Omega(m)} +p^ke^{-2\delta^2 N}
\\ & \le e^{-\Omega(m)} +e^{-\Omega(\delta^2 N)}
\\ & \le e^{-\Omega(m)} 
\end{align*}

Fourth, suppose $Q$ is constant, so we assume $Q(X)=2u+b$ for some $u\in \mathcal{U}$ and $b\in\{0, 1\}$, as $F_i(p-1)=0$ for every $i$. Now, if $B(u)=0$, then i.i.d. random bits $C_i(u)$ are used at every main coordinate $i$. Exactly as the third case, the probability of existing such $Q$ with $s(Q)\ge 1/2+\varepsilon$ is $e^{-\Omega(m)}$. 

Finally, it suffices to consider $Q(X)=2u+b$ where $B(u)=1$. Let $a=a(u)$, fix any realization of the source membership oracles $O=(O_1, \dots, O_m)$ from \cref{def:source-ensemble}, and let $r=r(a)$ be the fraction of constraints of the source instance $z=E(a)$ satisfies. We show that $s(Q)>1/2+\varepsilon$ forces $b=0$ and $r\notin[\eta,1-\eta]$. Fix any $b$ and $r$. Recall that for every $(J, h)\in \binom{[m]}{t}\times [H]$, by some parity arithmetic
\[
F_{J,h}(Q(\alpha_{J,h})) =F_{J,h}(2u+b) 
= \varphi_{J,h}(u)\oplus b
=
\mathbf{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left\{ \#\{j\in J: O_{j}(E_{j}(a))=0\} \text{ is even}\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right\}\oplus b.
\]
For active $u$, the value $F_{J,h}(2u+b)$ is independent of $h$. Every $t$-subset $J$ occurs exactly $H$ times among the main constraints, so averaging their satisfaction is the same as averaging over a uniform $J$. Now, we claim that
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\label{eq:consistency-constant}
s(2u)= \mathbb{P}_{J\in\binom{[m]}{t}}\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(\#\{j\in J: O_{j}(E_{j}(a))=0\} \text{ is even}\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right) \pm O\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(\frac{1}{n}\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)=\frac{1+(2r-1)^t}{2} \pm O\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(\frac{1}{n}+\frac{t^2}{m}\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right). \\
s(2u+1)= \mathbb{P}_{J\in\binom{[m]}{t}}\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(\#\{j\in J: O_{j}(E_{j}(a))=0\} \text{ is odd}\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right) \pm O\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(\frac{1}{n}\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)=\frac{1-(2r-1)^t}{2} \pm O\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(\frac{1}{n}+\frac{t^2}{m}\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right). \\
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The $O(1/n)$ term corresponds to constraints $F_O$ and $F_B$. We justify this inequality as follows: consider sampling $J$ instead as $t$ many i.i.d. uniform random indices $j_1, \dots, j_t\in[m]$, in which case the desired parity event---say the even case---is exactly the event that a $\operatorname{Bin}(t, 1-r)$ random variable is even, which has probability $1/2+(2r-1)^t/2$. Sampling with replacement in \cref{eq:consistency-constant}, the total variation is at most 
\[\mathbb{P}(j_1, \dots, j_t\text{ not distinct})\le \mathbb{P}(\exists\text{ distinct } t_1, t_2\in [t]:j_{t_1}=j_{t_2})\le \frac{t^2}{m}.\]
Using \cref{eq:consistency-constant}, we see that by our parameter choices \cref{eq:def-t,eq:def-p-new,eq:def-param}, $s(2u+1)\le 1/2+\varepsilon$ and that 
\[ s(Q)\le \frac{1+(1-2\eta)^t}{2}+O\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(\frac{1}{n}+\frac{t^2}{m}\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)< \frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon\]
whenever $r\in [\eta, 1-\eta]$. This depends on $O$ only through the event that $r(a(u))\in [\eta, 1-\eta]$.

Therefore, with probability $1-e^{-\Omega(m)}$ over the setup $S$, the first four steps hold, in which case we have that, for every realization $O$, the only $Q(X)$ with $s(Q)> 1/2+\varepsilon$ are $Q(X)=2u$ such that $B(u)=1$ and the satisfaction ratio of $E(a(u))$ on the source instance given by realization $O$ is less than $\eta$ or greater than $1-\eta$.
<!-- end proof-30 -->
<!-- theorem-15: body_md -->
\label{thm:balanced-opi-advantage}
Consider the ensemble in \cref{def:balanced-target}, with parameters
chosen in
\cref{eq:def-t,eq:def-p-new,eq:def-param}, every such OPI instance is balanced with rate
\[
R=\frac{k}{n}
=\Theta\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(
\frac{1}{(\log n)^4(\log\log n)^3}
\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)\quad\text{and}\quad
\log p=\Theta\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left((\log n)^4\log\log n\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right).
\]
as $m\to\infty$.
Under membership-oracle access to the input lists, the following hold:
\begin{enumerate}
\item There is a uniform quantum algorithm running in
polynomial time that outputs a polynomial $Q$ with
$\deg Q<k$ and $s(Q)=1$ with probability at least
\[
1-e^{-\Omega(m)}
=1-e^{-\Omega((\log n)^2)}.
\]

\item Every randomized classical algorithm
with polynomially many membership queries and unrestricted computation between
queries, and outputting
a polynomial $Q$ with $\deg Q<k$ satisfies
\[
\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(s(Q)>\frac12+\varepsilon\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)
\le e^{-\Omega(m)}
=e^{-\Omega((\log n)^2)}\quad\text{where}\quad \varepsilon
=\Theta\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(\frac{1}{\log\log n}\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right).
\]
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end theorem-15 -->
<!-- proof-31: body_md -->
The instances are balanced by definition. We first check the parameters. Since $t=\Theta(\log\log m)$,
\[
\binom mt\le m^t=2^{O(\log m\log\log m)}=2^{o(\sqrt m)},
\]
and so $n=N+2\sim2^{\sqrt m}$. Hence, failure probabilities are $e^{-\Omega(m)}
=e^{-\Omega((\log n)^2)}$, slack $\varepsilon =\Theta(1/\log\log n)$, and alphabet size $\log p=\Theta(m^2\log m)$. Finally,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:advantage-final-rate}
\frac{k}{n}
=\Theta\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(\frac{\delta^2}{\log p}\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)
=\Theta\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(\frac1{m^2(\log m)^3}\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)
=\Theta\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(\frac1{(\log n)^4(\log\log n)^3}\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right).
\end{equation}

For (1), we construct the quantum algorithm by solving the underlying instance from \cref{def:source-ensemble} via the quantum algorithm in \cref{thm:advantage-yz}. To simulate oracle query $O_j(z_j)$ for $j\in [m]$ and some $z_j\in \Sigma$, we efficiently find via \cref{eq:advantage-encoder} some $a\in \mathbb{F}_2^D$ with $E_j(a)=z_j$, compute
$u=u(j, a)\in \mathbb{F}_p$ via \cref{eq:advantage-embedding}, and return the query outcome
\[ F_O(2u) = O_{j(u)}(E_{j(u)}(a(u))) = O_j(z_j).\]

With probability $1-e^{-\Omega(m)}$, we find some perfects solution $z\in C_0$ to the corresponding instance in \cref{def:source-ensemble}, i.e. $O_j(z_j)=1$ for every $j$. Then, 
we find via \cref{eq:advantage-encoder} some $a\in \mathbb{F}_2^D$ with $E(a)=z$. Now, we test $u(j, a)=a+(j-1)2^D$ for $j=1, 2, \dots , \lceil m/\delta\rceil$ until we find $j$ where $B(u(j, a))=1$. We test this by computing $u(j, a)$ via \cref{eq:advantage-embedding} and querying
\[F_B(2u)=\varphi_B(u)=B(u).\]

Since $p\gg (m/\delta)2^D$, the $u(j, a)$ are distinct and they all belong to $\mathcal{U}$, so the probability that we cannot find some $j$ where $B(u(j, a))=1$ is at most
\[(1-\delta)^{\lceil m/\delta\rceil}\le e^{-m}\]
If we find some $j$ where $B(u(j, a))=1$, output the constant polynomial $Q(X)=2u(j, a)$. We see that this polynomial satisfies every constraint $F_i$ by \cref{prop:consistency}. 
Finally, the number of queries and all arithmetic are polynomial in $m$ and
$\log p$, hence polynomial in $n$.

For (2), fix any classical algorithm $\mathcal{A}$ for the target ensemble, making polynomially many target queries, say at most $n^d$ for a fixed $d$. We build a classical algorithm to solve the underlying source ensemble of \cref{def:source-ensemble}. To do so, we first sample a random setup $S=(\boldsymbol{\alpha}, B, C)$ as in \cref{def:balanced-target}. We sample gate and filler bits only when first needed and store them for repeated queries; all setup randomness is independent of $O$. With probability $1-e^{-\Omega(m)}$, the setup $S$ is \emph{consistent}, i.e. \cref{prop:consistency} holds. 

Now, fix any source instance given by any realization of oracles $O=(O_1, \dots, O_m)$, and solve it by running $\mathcal{A}$ on the target instance given by $O, S$ and simulating each classical target queries $F_i$ via $O$ as follows. We return $F_i(p-1)=0$ for every $i$. For a query $F_i(2u+b)$ with
$u\in\mathcal U$ and $b\in\{0,1\}$, compute $j(u),a(u)$ via
\cref{eq:advantage-parser} and proceed as follows:
\begin{itemize}
    \item If $i=O$, compute $E_{j(u)}(a(u))$ via
    \cref{eq:advantage-encoder}, make one source query to return
    \[
    O_{j(u)}(E_{j(u)}(a(u)))\oplus b = \varphi_O(u)\oplus b = F_O(2u+b).
    \]

    \item If $i=B$, return $B(u)\oplus b$. This requires only setup $S$ and no source query.

    \item If $i=(J,h)$, first inspect $B(u)$.
    If $B(u)=0$, return $C_{J,h}(u)\oplus b$.
    Otherwise, query $O_j(E_j(a(u)))$ for every $j\in J$ and compute the parity to return $F_i(2u+b)$ via \cref{eq:advantage-label-predicates}.
    This requires at most $t$ source queries.
\end{itemize}
Now, suppose $\mathcal{A}$ on this instance $(O, S)$ outputs polynomial $Q$, if $Q(X)=2u$
for some $u\in\mathcal U$, output $a(u)$; otherwise, output any fixed message
in $\mathbb{F}_2^D$. 

We analyze this algorithm on the source ensemble from \cref{def:source-ensemble} parametrized by $O$. 
This simulation has exactly the distribution of the target ensemble and uses at most
\[
tn^d
=2^{O_d(\sqrt m)}
<2^{m^{2/3}}-m
\]
source queries for sufficiently large $m$. 
By \cref{thm:advantage-yz}, the satisfaction ratio of the output must be in $[\eta, 1-\eta]$ with probability $1-e^{-\Omega(m)}$ over the source ensemble. 
By \cref{prop:consistency}, except on a setup event of probability
$e^{-\Omega(m)}$, consistency implies that every output with $s(Q)>1/2+\varepsilon$ satisfies $Q(X)=2u$ for some $B(u)=1$ and
$r(a(u))\in [0, \eta)\cup (1-\eta, 1]$.
Together, the output $Q$ of $\mathcal{A}$ has satisfaction ratio $s(Q)> 1/2+\varepsilon$ with probability at most $e^{-\Omega(m)}$. This holds for every algorithm $\mathcal{A}$ making polynomially many queries, proving (2).
<!-- end proof-31 -->
<!-- remark-10: body_md -->
\label{rem:alternative-param}
By tuning the parameter choices, we obtain an analogous version of \cref{thm:balanced-opi-advantage} as follows: Let $\varepsilon>0$ be any absolute constant.  Then we may choose any sufficiently large fixed even $t$ satisfying \cref{eq:def-t}, rather than the least such $t$. If we take $H=1$ and $N=\binom{m}{t}$, then $C$ has length $n\asymp m^t$ and rate
\[
R=\Theta_{\varepsilon,t}\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(\frac1{m^2\log m}\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)
=\Theta_{\varepsilon,t}\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(\frac{n^{-2/t}}{\log n}\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right).
\]
Since $n$ is now polynomial in $m$ (rather than $2^{\Theta(\sqrt m)}$), hardness of polynomial-query classical algorithms (rather than $2^{O(\sqrt m)}$ queries) for the source ensemble \cref{def:source-ensemble} suffices for this target ensemble. In this version, for any constant $\varepsilon>0$ and $c>0$, choosing also $t>2/c$ gives such a target ensemble with field size $p=\exp(n^{\Theta(1)})$, rate $R\gg n^{-c}$, polynomial-query classical algorithms have ceiling $1/2+\varepsilon$, and error probability $e^{-\Omega(m)}=e^{-\Omega(n^{1/t})}$.
<!-- end remark-10 -->
<!-- example-2: body_md -->
Take $\ell_i(x)=\mathbf{1}\{x=0\}$, $v=0$,
and $v'\notin C$. The all-one transcript has probabilities
$p^{-k}$ and zero under the two translates, but probability
$p^{-n}$ under $\nu$, giving
\[
\mathsf{F}_{\operatorname{LLR}}(0,v')\ge\frac14p^{n-2k}.
\]
which blows up when rate $R=k/n<1/2$.
<!-- end example-2 -->
<!-- lemma-20: body_md -->
\label{lem:centered-l1-llr} 
Define $c_i:=2\sqrt{\rho_i(1-\rho_i)}$  for each $i\in [n]$. For every
$v,v'\in\mathbb{F}_p^n$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:centered-l1-llr}
2d_{\mathrm{TV}}(L_v,L_{v'})
\le
\sum_{\emptyset\ne A\subset[n]}
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(\prod_{i\in A}c_i\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft|
\sum_{\substack{y\in C^\perp\\\operatorname{supp}(y)=A}}
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft[e_p(-\langle v,y\rangle)-e_p(-\langle v',y\rangle)\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright]
\prod_{i\in A}\widehat g_i(y_i)
\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright|.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-20 -->
<!-- proof-32: body_md -->
By \cref{eq:llr-proxy-key} and the triangle inequality,
\begin{align*}
2d_{\mathrm{TV}}(L_v,L_{v'})
&=
\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim\nu}
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft|\frac{\mathbb{P}(L_v=Z)-\mathbb{P}(L_{v'}=Z)}{\nu(Z)}\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright|\\
&\le
\sum_{\emptyset\ne A\subset[n]}
\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim\nu}\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft|\psi_A(Z)\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright|
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft|
\sum_{\substack{y\in C^\perp\\\operatorname{supp}(y)=A}}
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft[e_p(-\langle v,y\rangle)-e_p(-\langle v',y\rangle)\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright]
\prod_{i\in A}\widehat g_i(y_i)
\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright|.
\end{align*}
The empty-support term vanishes. Since $\nu$ is a product distribution,
\[
\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim\nu}\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft|\psi_A(Z)\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright|
=
\prod_{i\in A}\mathbb{E}_{Z_i\sim\nu_i}\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\originalleft|\psi_i(Z_i)\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\originalright|
=
\prod_{i\in A}
\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(
\rho_i\sqrt{\frac{1-\rho_i}{\rho_i}}
+
(1-\rho_i)\sqrt{\frac{\rho_i}{1-\rho_i}}
\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)
=
\prod_{i\in A}c_i.
\]
Substituting this identity proves \cref{eq:centered-l1-llr}.
<!-- end proof-32 -->
<!-- lemma-21: body_md -->
\label{lem:bl-mds}
Let $C\subset\mathbb{F}_p^n$ be an $[n,k]$ MDS code of rate $R=k/n$, and
let $u\in\mathbb{F}_p^n$. Then, for arbitrary functions
$h_1,\ldots,h_n:\mathbb{F}_p\to\mathbb{R}_{\ge0}$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:bl-mds-normalized}
    \mathbb{E}_{x\in u+C}\prod_{i=1}^n h_i(x_i)
    \le
    \prod_{i=1}^n
    \mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(\mathbb{E}_{a\in\mathbb{F}_p}h_i(a)^{1/R}\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)^R.
\end{equation}
Equivalently, writing $\|\cdot\|_{1/R}$ for the unnormalized
$\ell^{1/R}(\mathbb{F}_p)$ norm,
\[
    \sum_{x\in u+C}\prod_{i=1}^n h_i(x_i)
    \le
    \prod_{i=1}^n\|h_i\|_{1/R}.
\]
<!-- end lemma-21 -->
<!-- proof-33: body_md -->
Every coordinate belongs to exactly $\binom{n-1}{k-1}
    =
    R\binom nk$
sets in $\binom{[n]}k$. Hence
\[
    \prod_{i=1}^n h_i(x_i)
    =
    \prod_{J\in\binom{[n]}k}
    \mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(\prod_{j\in J}h_j(x_j)\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)^{
        1/\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(R\binom nk\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)}.
`\]
Applying H\"older's inequality with exponent $\binom nk$ to each of the
$\binom nk$ factors gives
\[
    \mathbb{E}_{x\in u+C}\prod_{i=1}^n h_i(x_i)
    \le
    \prod_{J\in\binom{[n]}k}
    \mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(
      \mathbb{E}_{x\in u+C}
      \prod_{j\in J}h_j(x_j)^{1/R}
    \aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)^{1/\binom nk}.
\]
Since $C$ is MDS, projection onto every $J\in\binom{[n]}k$ is a
bijection from $u+C$ to $\mathbb{F}_p^J$. Therefore
\[
    \mathbb{E}_{x\in u+C}
    \prod_{j\in J}h_j(x_j)^{1/R}
    =
    \prod_{j\in J}
    \mathbb{E}_{a\in\mathbb{F}_p}h_j(a)^{1/R}.
\]
Substituting this identity and using that each coordinate belongs to
an $R$ fraction of the sets in $\binom{[n]}k$, we obtain
\[
    \mathbb{E}_{x\in u+C}\prod_{i=1}^n h_i(x_i)
    \le
    \prod_{J\in\binom{[n]}k}
    \mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(
      \prod_{j\in J}
      \mathbb{E}_{a\in\mathbb{F}_p}h_j(a)^{1/R}
    \aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)^{1/\binom nk} =
    \prod_{i=1}^n
    \mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\mathopen{}\mathclose\bgroup\left(\mathbb{E}_{a\in\mathbb{F}_p}h_i(a)^{1/R}\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\aftergroup\egroup\right)^R.
\]
This proves \cref{eq:bl-mds-normalized}. Multiplying by
$|C|=p^k=p^{Rn}$ gives the equivalent unnormalized formulation.
<!-- end proof-33 -->
<!-- lemma-22: body_md -->
\label{lem:arbitrary-llr-below}
There exists an absolute constant $\delta>0$ such that for every generalized Reed-Solomon code $C\subset\mathbb{F}_p^n$ of fixed rate $R\ge1/2-\delta$ over a prime
field $\mathbb{F}_p$ with $p\ge n$, and every one-bit leakage tuple, uniformly over $v,v'\in\mathbb{F}_p^n$,
\[
d_{\mathrm{TV}}(L_v,L_{v'})\le e^{-\Omega(n)}.
\]
<!-- end lemma-22 -->
<!-- proof-34: body_md -->
For $R>1/2$, it follows \cref{cor:one-bit-above}, so we fix
$R\in[1/2-\delta,1/2]$. Fix $q_0<1$ supplied by \cref{lem:u3-gap} with $\tau=1/4$. Complementing individual leaked bits,
we assume $\rho_i\ge1/2$. Recall that
\[
\mathbb{E}|g_i|^2=1,
\qquad
c_i\|g_i\|_\infty=2\rho_i\le2.
\]
We choose $\varepsilon>0$ sufficiently small and then
$\delta>0$ sufficiently small depending on $\varepsilon$.
Define support cutoff $m:=\lfloor(2-\delta)k\rfloor$.
By \cref{lem:centered-l1-llr} and Fourier inversion,
\[
d_{\mathrm{TV}}(L_v,L_{v'})
